A.21. Executive Summary

A.21.1 Project/Programme Executive Summary

1. Project 1 is intended to be embedded in a broader programme (see Section A.22), providing a strategic opportunity to achieve a paradigm shift in the forest and land-use sector of Lao PDR.
2. Laos has embraced REDD+ as a way to address its primary source of GHG emissions. Laos has been working with the World Bank since 2013 on the development of a National Forest Strategy (NFS) under the Forest Investment Facility (FIF) in 2017. Lao PDR was accepted as one of the eight pilot countries for the Forest Investment Programme (FIP) in 2018, and has since received €4 million from the FIP to support its NFS development.
3. In recent years, Laos has progressively substituted towards REDD+ readiness, notably through the acceptance of the Lao PDR National Strategy on Climate Change (NSCC), the Forest Management Plan (FMP) under the Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF), as well as the final acceptance of the Emission Reductions from Avoided Deforestation (ERAD) mechanism. The country has also been working towards ambitious NDC targets (70% forest cover), a timber export ban, and a new Forest Law.
4. Lao PDR is now strategically positioned to take advantage of the FCPF Carbon Fund (FCPF CF) as it aligns with the country’s climate change priorities and national development goals.

* The GO2 Letter of Internal Approval is provided in Annex 15.

A well-designed framework for Phase 2 of REDD+ (implementation) in order to reduce forest-sector emissions and to achieve true paradigm shift.

### 4. To achieve such a paradigm shift, the programme in which Project 1 is embedded:

- **Strengthens the enabling environment for REDD+:** Through 3 principal means: enhancing the availability of financial resources; strengthening the institutional and policy framework supporting a REDD+ Funding Window; reviving and strengthening the legal and regulatory framework for forest conservation and sustainable management.
- **Supports deforestation-free agriculture and agriforestry:** By enhancing productivity, increasing farmers' integration into markets, improving access to credit, diversifying sources of finance and senior sector participation in economic activities that reduce pressure on forests.
- **Supports landscape restoration (LFR) on over 1.5 million ha of degraded lands:**

#### 5. The programme consists of 3 projects:
Project 1 (mid-2020 to mid-2024): addresses the three provinces of Houaphan, Bolikhamxay and Khammouane in Laos. It is a stand-alone project with no direct coordination within the programme area.
Project 2 (mid-2024 to end-2021): scales up the number of participating communities in the project area from 35 to 67, thereby increasing the geographical reach of the programme to the 3 additional provinces: Luang Namtha, Bokeo and Oudomxay.
Project 3 (mid-2024 to end-2021): scales up the number of participating communities in the project area from 35 to 67, thereby increasing the geographical reach of the programme to the 3 additional provinces: Luang Namtha, Bokeo and Oudomxay.

#### 6. The programme structure offers considerable flexibility through its constituent projects to gather data and learn lessons on how best to scale up REDD+ in Laos. It also provides a platform for exploring opportunities from diversified sources (more emission reductions, additional domestic revenues, larger endowment for the REDD+ Funding Window, etc.). Project 1 is designed as a stand-alone project with no direct coordination within the programme area.
This Funding Proposal presents a stand-alone GCF project (Project 1) for Board approval. Two subsequent stand-alone projects, embedded in the same programmatic context and theory of change as this project, will be submitted to the Board at a later stage.

#### A2.1 Project 1 Executive Summary

- **Project 1 mitigates:** 5.6 million tCO2e during its 4-year implementation period, at a cost to the GCF of Euro 2.7/tonneCO2e.
- **It benefits:**
    - 355,800 people (120,000 directly) in 3 provinces of rural northern Laos by promoting sustainable land use and forest management practices.
    - 46,000 ha of degraded lands through landscape restoration activities.
- **Project 1 mobilises Euro:** 50.0 million tCO2e of co-financing and will, during its 4-year implementation period, unlock REDD+ results-based payments of Euro 23 million from the FCPF Carbon Fund.

#### Project 1 will have significant socio-economic and gender-positive co-benefits in disadvantaged rural areas. Laos REDD+ is a flagship project for the GCF, and this project can act as a pilot for other projects to come. Although the government is contributing significant co-financing, surpassing its NDC commitments, the GCF's contribution is essential for Project 1 to proceed.

# A2.2 Programme summary

## A2.2.1 Programme structure

A more detailed proposal is sought solely for Project 1 and not for the overall programme (i.e., not for constituent Project 2 or constituent Project 3), a summary of the overall programme, in which Project 1 is embedded, is provided below for reference.

The programme as proposed in this document is embedded, contains a combined sectoral and geographical screen:

- It is sectoral in the sense that it supports the Government of Lao PDR in achieving its policy goals, as defined in the NDC and as well as the National REDD+ Strategy and Socio-Economic Development Plan.
- It is geographical in the sense that it covers a number of provinces in Lao PDR. The selection was based on the basis of their mitigation potential under the ER Program. The programme applies a geographical variation in terms of project design, implementation, and monitoring and reporting. Projects are implemented at ecosystem level and that are linked through other factors such as synergies among co-financing activities.

The programme design consists of:

- Three Projects:
  - Project 1: Houaphan, Sayaboury and Luang Prabang provinces. Mid-2020 to mid-2024.
  - Project 2: Luang Namtha, Bokeo and Oudomxay provinces. 2022 to end-2029
  - Project 3: Xieng Khouang, Phonsavan and Luang Prabang provinces. Mid-2024 to end-2029.

Each Project contains three Outputs:
- Output 1: Creation of an enabling environment for REDD+
- Output 2: Development of a carbon market information system
- Output 3: Climate mitigation action through forestry

Project 1 addresses a number of national-level interventions that will also serve to support Projects 2 and 3 (subject to separate funding). These include, but are not limited to, the establishment of an enabling environment for REDD+ Funding Window, which will be undertaken in Project 1 but whose structure and functionality will also be utilised in Projects 2 and 3.

At the core of the project are performance-based payments to participating communities and institutions to incentivise and fund those types of land-use practices that yield the highest emission reductions. The programme seeks to build on the experience gained from the design, implementation and monitoring of the pilot phase of Project 1 and Project 3, for which additional funding may be approved subsequently by the GCF Board.

## A2.2.2 Benefits of the programmatic approach


---
**Page 9**
---

16. The Feasibility Study (Annex B) recommends a programmatic approach instead of a static project approach. The programmatic approach will allow:

- Phased geographical upscaling of activities through a sequenced series of interlinked projects for different regions/programmes in the ERP accounting area.
- A REDD+ Fund programme with a strong emphasis on local and national institutional capacity at the centre of the programme.
- Common objectives aligning the constituent projects and contributing to the strategic goals of the forestry element of LAC countries.
- A stronger emphasis on performance-based financing.
- A more flexible approach, allowing for scaling up activities beyond a single project.
- Scaling-up project activities while building up the capacity of LAC institutions.
- Faster implementation through phased geographical upscaling and programme progress in years.

17. The benefits of a programmatic approach include:

- Impact: Greater flexibility through constituent projects to gather data and learn iteratively for more effective and adaptive design and to achieve higher impact in subsequent projects (built on know-how and lessons learned).
- Sustainability: Higher potential for crowding-in more sustainable investments from alternative financing sources (more investment opportunities for the private sector) and for the REED+ funding mechanism, including a hybrid financing mechanism at the entire programme and thereby increasing long-term financial sustainability.
- Participatory approach: More inclusive and participatory approach to project design and implementation through phased geographical upscaling in the land-use sector through the extended duration of the programme.
- Efficiency: Reduced transaction costs for the Fund and its partners, as well as reduced costs over time, in particular through the common financing mechanism (the REED+ Funding Window).
- Standardization: More standardization of project design and implementation across the ERP Program.
- Coherence: A more open platform for coordination and harmonization of existing co-financing contributions (AFD, etc.) with the LAC towards the NDC forestry objectives, as well as a vehicle for attracting additional co-financing.

18. All three constituent projects of the programme contribute to reducing emissions and increasing carbon sequestration in forest ecosystems of the accounting area. All funded activities in each project have the purpose of contributing directly or indirectly towards achieving the framework conditions to implement, sustain and safeguard those activities that contribute directly to this goal.

19. The constituent projects complement each other to achieve outcomes beyond those that could be achieved by landowners acting alone. These include:

- Combining the mitigation potential, and therefore the potential for accessing results-based payments, at the scale necessary to sustain mitigation activities without subsequent programme support.
- Promoting landscape connectivity
- Exchange and learning between participating stakeholders.

The constituent projects apply common implementation arrangements, including a:
- Joint Programme Management Structure
- Centralised national financial mechanism (the REDD+ Funding Window) to allocate project financing and results-based payments through the FCPF Development Fund
- Common Environmental and Social Frameworks, including a Management Plan and an Ethnic Groups Organisations’ Agreement
- Common monitoring framework.

A programme is, therefore, more efficient and effective in terms of both its costs and impacts.


### A.24 Programme Area

20. The envisaged programme, in which Project 1 is embedded, serves as the cornerstone instrument for successful implementation of the Land Emission Reduction Programme (ERP). Both cover the same area: provinces in northern Lake Victoria sub-region, including Bokwe and Luang Namtha Provinces in Laos, and parts of Kedong District and the areas in Arunachal Pradesh. The programme location constitutes 30% of the total degraded forest land in the region and majority’s forest cover is located within the same geographical area as the ERP. Its ecological characteristics are similar to those of the ERP and will directly unlock FCPF results-based payments.

#### Project 1 and Project 3 address the three provinces of Houayxay, Sayaboury and Luang Prabang. These provinces contain the districts with the highest rates of deforestation and degradation (50% of total degraded forest land in Laos). The programme will focus on areas at risk of deforestation / forest degradation.

In addition, the geographical locations of these three provinces enable connectivity of forest ecosystems at a landscape level.


#### Project 1 (mid-2020 to mid-2024) and Project 3 (mid-2024 to end-2029) will be implemented consecutively. This is because the programme has been designed in such a way that it can only be fully operational once all the FCPF results-based payments are available for disbursal. The programme will be embedded into the context of a more managed landscape and will benefit from lessons learned in Project 1, which aims to avoid overexploitation by allowing implementation of community capacities of partners before scaling up to Project 3.

The programme is designed such that it can only be fully operational once all the FCPF results-based payments are available for disbursal. The programme will be embedded into the context of a more managed landscape and will benefit from lessons learned in Project 1, which aims to avoid overexploitation by allowing implementation of community capacities of partners before scaling up to Project 3.

The programme is which Projects are embedded to be in a position to access additional results-based payments. The programme will be implemented in three stages: Stage I (2020-2024) will see the number of participating communities increase to 400 in the same geographical area (Project 3 as well as adjacent areas). Stage II (2025-2029) will see this number increase to 600. Stage III (2030-2034) will see it increase further.

#### Project 2 (2022 to end-2029) addresses the three remaining provinces of Luang Namtha, Bokeo and Choua Boun. The programme in these provinces is designed such that it can only be fully operational once all FCPF results-based payments are available for disbursal.

The programme will be embedded to benefit from lessons learned in Project 1 when expanding the geographical scope of the programme into Luang Namtha, Bokeo and Choua Boun Provinces. The programme will focus on degraded land use plans in 103 villages in Bokeo and Luang Namtha provinces up to 2021, when KFW support is scheduled to phase out.

These stages are target areas for Project 2.

# PROJECT INFORMATION

## B.1. Context and background

### B.1.1. Context

24. The Lao People's Democratic Republic (Lao PDR) is a landlocked least developed country (LLDC) in Southeast Asia. The country covers an area of 24 million hectares, has a population of 6.9 million* and an average per capita income of USD $300 (World Bank, 2015). Lao PDR is one of the world's most forested countries with approximately 78% of its land covered by forests (FAO, 2014). The country's economy is dependent on natural resources, especially forestry, agriculture, electricity generation and distribution, mining, agriculture, forestry and fisheries account for 65% of GDP and employ 64% of the labor force.

The agricultural sector consists primarily of subsistence farming and is characterized by low yields due to limited access to inputs such as fertilizers, pesticides, seeds, and irrigation. Significant crops include rice, maize, sugarcane and cassava. Some 80% of the population are heavy reliant on forests for their livelihoods (FAO, 2014). Lao PDR is a biodiversity hotspot with approximately one-third of its flora recognized as globally threatened. Forest cover accounts for 59% of the country's surface area, and forest degradation and deforestation are major environmental challenges in the country. The total forested land area was estimated to approximate 280,000 ha, the forest cover (175 seconds per hectare) is dominated by regenerating forests, with a significant proportion of forest vegetation characterized by succession and size structure, and loss of wildlife and plant habitats – as is concerning as deforestation.

### B.1.2. Key challenges

Lao PDR's greenhouse gas emissions from land use change and forestry have increased from 34 to 34.1 million tCO2-equivalent in the period 2005-2015, with an increasing trend in 2010–2015. Carbon removals due to reforestation and afforestation activities are estimated at approximately 6.7 million tCO2-equivalent per year. The average GHG emissions of 38.6 million tCO2 from land use change and forestry in the period 2005-2015 is attributed mainly to land use change (responsible for 67% of Lao PDR's emissions), and agriculture contributes a further 28%.

In its Second National Communication to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC) in 2013, Lao PDR identified climate change as one of the major challenges facing the country due to climate change impacts. The country is considered to have a high risk of river flooding, landslides, cyclones and droughts. These natural hazards are expected to increase in frequency and intensity with climate change. Different groups disproportionately face climate risks, among them temperature increases and erratic rainfall, given that they are more vulnerable due to poverty, lack of access to information and resources, and limited capacity to adapt from the immediate environment. Although this GCF proposal is a mitigation (REDD+) initiative, the proposal incorporates interventions that will promote climate change adaptation – notably enhanced land-use planning, improvements to irrigation infrastructure, reforestation in catchment areas and Climate-smart agriculture.

# B

## 8.1.2 Policy Framework

The importance of forest resources and their sustainable management is enshrined in the country's high-level policies, including the 8th National Socio-Economic Development Plan (8th NSEDP, 2016-2020), the Green Growth Strategy (GGS) (2015-2030), the National Forest Policy (NFP) (2017), and the Forestry Strategy 2020, as well as in Lao PDR's Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC).

### 8.1.2.1 NDC Targets

Lao’s NDC places significant weight on the forestry sector, establishing a forest cover target of 70% as well as additional targets under the category of “Improved Forest Management.” The underlying Forestry Strategy 2020 outlines the following sector targets:

- Improve the quality of forests by naturally regenerating up to six million and planting (including through afforestation) an additional two million hectares of forest land, with a particular focus on the establishment of a rural livelihoods support system encompassing watershed water supplies and prevention of natural disasters (mainly floods).
- Provide a sustainable flow of forest products for domestic consumption and to generate revenue through value-added processing. This will also contribute to the country’s foreign exchange earnings while increasing direct and indirect employment.
- Promote afforestation, reforestation, and rehabilitation in areas affected by natural disasters under emergency conditions, when necessary.
- Conserve the environment, including protection of soil, conservation of watersheds and combating climate change.

### 8.1.2.2 National REDD+ Strategy (NRES)

In 2016, the government complemented the NDC and Forestry Strategy with the National REDD+ Strategy (NRES) to 2035 and National REDD+ Vision to 2030 as the official government documents for guiding REDD+ implementation in Lao PDR. The NRES is a comprehensive strategy that sets out a series of policies, measures, and actions rationalized to provide economic, social, and environmental benefits. The policies require all stakeholders, including households, communities, local authorities, provincial governments, and the private sector, to reduce deforestation and degradation, and the promotion of forest restoration and reforestation.

### 8.1.2.3 Lao’s REDD+ Journey

Lao has been a partner in the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF) since 2008. Its Readiness Preparation Proposal (RPP) was approved by the FCPF Board in March 2015, and its Emission Reductions Programme Document (ERPD) was accepted into the FCPF portfolio on 30 June 2016. The Lao REDD+ Action Plan (LRAP), which is a key component of the ERP, is the articulation of the government’s comprehensive strategy to reduce GHG emissions and increase carbon sinks in the country. The LRAP was developed by the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment, Luang Namtha, Bokeo and Oudomxay – that comprise the Lao Emission Reductions Programme (Area) Project (LERPA). The LRAP is a multi-sectoral strategy that covers all sectors of the economy and shares an identical target area (and accounting system) as the ERP. This six-strategy project of the LRAP is an aggregation of the processes of REDD+ Action Plans (RAPs) developed for each target province in the period 2016-2035.

### 8.1.2.4 Government Reforms

The government, together with development partners, has put in place extensive institutional framework at national and provincial levels to support the implementation of REDD+ activities. This includes the establishment of a National REDD+ Task Force representing diverse economic sectors, including forestry, agriculture, energy, water resources, land use planning, environment, and finance. The task force is chaired by the Ministry of Finance, Ministry of Planning and Investment, the Lao Front for National Development (LFND), the Lao Women’s Union, and the Lao Red Cross Society. It also includes representatives from the legal framework, land tenure, MRV, safeguards, benefit-sharing, and enforcement and implementation of REDD+ activities in the country.

In parallel, the government has, in recent years, embarked on a number of reforms that provide a platform for launching GCF project interventions. In 2012, the government issued a suspension on granting of new concessions for mining, eucalyptus and rubber investments, which remains in effect today. A national

B

monoculture logging in production forests has also been in effect since 2013 in order to protect natural forests from unsustainable exploitation.** In 2016, the government put in place measures to halt illegal logging and illegal timber trade. These include the Forest Industry Act (FIA) of 2015, the Voluntary Partnership Agreement (VPA) negotiations with the EU,** the adoption of the Central Party Committee Resolution on Law No. 38 of 2016, and the amendment to the Forestry Law in 2019. The Forestry Law (2007), which determines the basic principles, regulations and measures for the use of forest resources, was amended by the Government Decision No. 45/2019/QD-TTg dated January 30, 2019 Acting in the sector. Moreover, since 2015 all responsibilities for forestry have been returned to one ministry, the Ministry of Agriculture and Rural Development (MARD).

In summary, Lao PDR is positioned to embark upon an ambitious transformation of its forest sector. The NDC establishes the government's climate action framework and accords forestry and agriculture prominent role. The National REDD+ Strategy (NRS) sets out a comprehensive strategy for the country’s transition towards a low forest sector. The ERP is the key government initiative to operationalize the National REDD+ Strategy, and Project 1 addresses the three provinces with the highest rates of deforestation and forest degradation within the ERP accounting area.

As outlined below, the project is explicitly designed around the ERP**, sharing the same target geographical area and a common set of indicators. The project will also contribute to the implementation of the NRS by providing payments based on forests from the ERP and to channel the revenues from these payments back to stakeholders for implementing sustainable forest practices.
## B.1 Project Area

The northern region of Laos is characterized by hilly topography, remote accessibility and limited public and industrial infrastructure, unique ethnic communities and a persistent prevalence of poverty. Project 1 addresses the three provinces with the highest rates of deforestation and forest degradation within the ERP accounting area and contain the remaining intact forests in the country. The project will contribute to the implementation of the NRS by providing payments based on forests from the ERP and to channel the revenues from these payments back to stakeholders for implementing sustainable forest practices.

Within these three provinces, the project will serve approximately 200 villages in the following 16 districts: Huay Xai District, Phongsali Province; Nam Ou District, Xieng Khouang, Sopboua District; Phonsavan District, Houaphan Province; Phonsavan District, Luang Prabang Province; Nan, Phonhong, Phonsavan, Viengxay, Vientiane XeNgeun

The selection process for the project area was based on a set of quantitative considerations, which are described in Chapter 2.5 of the Feasibility Study (Annex 2) and are shown in Annex 16. For each selected district, a detailed risk assessment was conducted to identify those districts with the highest potential impact. The selection process focused on areas with high rates of deforestation and forest degradation. These three provinces have the most intact forests at a landscape level.

### B.1.4 Base Line Scenario: Emissions from, Drivers of, Deforestation and Forest Degradation

The project area was selected based on data collected thoroughly as part of Lao PDR's engagement with REDD+ (the UNFCCC Forest Reference Emission Level [FREL] and the FCPF Carbon Fund [FCPF CF]) and the Government’s National Strategy for Sustainable Development. The project area is located 10 years from the forest sector in the six northern provinces that constitute the programme area, driven primarily by forest degradation, deforestation and other drivers of emissions (CO2eq/year), which are only partially offset by slight gains from reforestation (-1.5m CO2eq/year) and forest restoration (-1.3m CO2eq/year).


---
**Page 15**
---

## 30. Shifting Cultivation is the greatest single proximate driver of deforestation and forest degradation in the project area, responsible for 22% of forest disturbances greater than 5 hectares in size. Agricultural land expansion (19%) and plantations (4%), both driven by shifting cultivation, are also major drivers since land conversion includes non-forestation (12%), selective logging (10%), and the establishment of tree plantations (3%).

### Environmental impacts:
* Erosion: With increasing demand for land to grow crops, fallow periods become shorter. When fallow periods fail, forests become degraded, leading to lower productivity, increased soil degradation and the need to clear new areas.
* Soil fertility: Intensive cultivation of annual crops on abandoned subsistence crop-into forest areas through slash-and-burn practices is typically a last resort among poor farmers with no alternative options. In this respect, shifting cultivation practices are not a safe net for poor and vulnerable groups.

### Agro-technological factors:
* Upland rice yields are often limited by seasonal precipitation, as the crop is highly rain-dependent.
* Intensive use of fertilizers and pesticides in upland rice production has led to soil degradation. Continued planting of upland rice without intercropping can lead to significant reductions in soil fertility – as much as 80% over a period of five years or more – due to nutrient depletion, leaching, and erosion after clearance.
* After a few years, the productivity declines again and places pressure on other forest areas.

### Policy and institutional factors:
* Inefficient and inappropriate land use planning is a major underlying cause of deforestation. The lack of enforcement in upland areas has led to illegal logging and conversion into plantations (which, in the past, have often been designed in a top-down manner involving limited consultation with villagers). This has resulted in forest degradation.
* Land tenure insecurity: In many villages, land use plans have been developed without adequate incentives mechanisms to encourage improved land management practices. The lack of enforcement of land use plans is also a major cause of non-compliance with land use plans which is weak in many villages and districts, and often areas under cultivation are underutilized or abandoned.
* Lack of government commitment: In some cases, the implementation of forest protection remains a challenge. Land allocation practices, especially in rural areas, have been hindered by a lack of government commitment.

## 41. The expansion of agricultural land (permanent agriculture) and plantation agriculture in the project area is driven by:
* Economic factors: Strong regional markets, especially in neighboring countries, continue to drive the production of key export agricultural commodities such as rubber, sugar cane, maize and cassava.
* Demand from large-scale producers: In many cases, smallholder farmers are unable to meet demand for their produce. Local SMEs play an important role in these supply chains, as they typically act as traders between Lao producers and Chinese buyers.

In Chapter 2 of the Feasibility Study (Annex A), cultivation of cash crops is seen as a direct route out of poverty for households with access to land and credit. However, the lack of infrastructure, including roads and markets are important for economic development in Lao PDR: weak land use planning, law enforcement and agricultural extension services are also major constraints.

### Agro-technological factors:
* The use of low-yield crop varieties.
* Lack of appropriate management practices and nitrogen loss due to soil continuing pollution of crops lead to additional forest being cleared for agriculture. While many farmers have access to improved seeds and fertilizers, the lack of extension services associated with improved maize varieties and agricultural inputs, challenges associated with mono-cropping on steep slopes are still common.

* **Policy and institutional factors:** Land use plans and targets established in provincial and district Socio-Economic Development Plans (SEDPs) often differ from, and are not aligned with, the land use policies and practices that exist on the ground. This is due to a lack of enforcement mechanisms for policy and regulations. For instance, the SEDP for Huaphong province has established an official target for avoided deforestation of 105,243 ha. However, the actual area covered by REDD+ targets results in a total agricultural area that is three times larger than the provided target. Inconsistencies in major land use plans and policies are due to the cause of deforestation; as these plans are not reflective of the actual land use activities that are implemented.

* **The primary barriers to sustainable forest management and reforestation include gaps in forest governance and regulation, lack of enforcement mechanisms for policy and regulations, and a lack of capacity at all levels.** This includes limited knowledge and experience in sustainable forest management. Detailed analysis of these barriers can be found in Chapter 2 of the Feasibility Study (Annex 2a).

## B.1.5 Baseline Project

A number of projects have been, and continue to be, implemented under the Project 1 target region. Extensive consultations have been undertaken with these projects; the design of Project 1 builds upon their lessons learned and experience gained.

### Climate Protection through Avoided Deforestation (CIPAD): Government of Cambodia (BMZ through GIZ) and World Bank

The CIPAD project is a joint initiative between the government of Cambodia, the World Bank, and the government of Laos PDR in its efforts to conclude a bilateral voluntary partnership agreement (VPA) with the EU on the basis of which the VPA will be implemented. The project aims at supporting the development of the measures to be undertaken by both parties to combat legal logging. CIPAD includes the development of underpinning legislation and regulations, as well as capacity building for law enforcement agencies. Furthermore, it represents an essential contribution to Activities 1.4 (forestry law reform), 2.1 (deforestation-free agricultural production), 3.5 (land use planning), 6.1 (forest governance), 7.1 (law enforcement and trade), 8.1 (capacity building for TULs) and TULs, and defines what constitutes legally-produced timber. How to control the supply chain, how to verify legally-sourced timber.

### Village Forest Management (VFM): Government of Germany (BMZ through KfW), 2015-2025

The project is an improvement of forest ecosystems and the livelihoods of the people living in the project area. The VFM project will be implemented as a joint initiative between the government of Cambodia, and close cooperation within Activity 3.1 of Project 1 (see below). The VFM pilot districts are also Project 1 target districts: Phnom Penh, Kampong Speu, Stung Treng, and Mondulkiri. The project is aligned with the government’s commitment in the districts of Phnom Penh and Phnom and is reflected in the budget for Project 1 since no budget from the GCF is available to finance this project.

### Land Management and Decentralisation Planning (LMDP): Government of Germany (BMZ through GIZ), 2015-2016

The LMDP project objective is to improve policies, practices and planning processes in relation to land use in Laos PDR. The project will provide technical assistance for the development of a national framework of policy and provide advice to the Laa Government on improving land governance (i.e., land use and spatial planning), land tenure security, and forest law enforcement. The LMDP project is closely linked with Activity 3.1 of Project 1 (see below). The LMDP project also focuses on registration of plots of land.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL (2.0) PAGE 13 OF 95

## Integrated Conservation of Biodiversity and Forests (ICBF): Government of Germany (BMZ through KfW), 2017-2022

The ICBF project builds on the experience gained from the previous phase, which focused on improving protected national areas (NPAs) and corridors that sustain biodiversity in forest ecosystems, while supporting livelihoods. The project will continue to work with communities in the same areas as Project 1 (Lusaka, Namibia and Bokwe - and will continue activities there until 2022). The components are: (i) improved planning and management of NPAs and corridors; (ii) sustainable land use and forest management, including livelihood activities based on participatory land use planning (PLUP); and (iii) capacity building for the implementation of REDD+ in NPAs. Component 1 builds upon the experience of Project 1 built up from the ICBF project and its approaches on capacity building, strengthening data/information gathering and analysis, and monitoring and evaluation. Component 2 builds on the experience gained under the ICBF project and will focus on sustainable land use and forest management activities (Activity 2.1 and 2.2), law enforcement (Activity 1.4) and coordination with village development funds (Activity 2.3).

## Incentives Mechanisms for Private Sector Engagement Under REDD+ in Laos PDR: (AICF)-Government of Canada, 2017-2025

The AICF project aims to support the Government of Laos PDR (GLOP) in implementing a national REDD+ strategy and policy framework. The project will also provide technical assistance for the development of policies and mechanisms that incentivize deforestation-free agriculture (including agroforestry). Investments will be generated from the work that provides incentives for private sector engagement.

## Sustainable Forest Management and REDD+ Support Project (F-REDD): Government of Japan (JICA), 2014-2025

The F-REDD project aims to strengthen the capacity of the Lao forestry sector through strengthening policies, institutions, and human resources. The project will also support the implementation of a national REDD+ process, and enhance REDD+ readiness in a pilot site. The F-REDD activities are closely linked to Project 1 (Lusaka, Namibia, Bokwe and Odzambo) and will be implemented under the supervision of the GLOP and the JICA also will co-finance the implementation of the project. The project will support the development of a national forest management framework and institutional interventions to enhance the FRL.

## Sustainable Forestry for Rural Development (SFRD): DAAD/Ministry of Finance, 2013-2018

The SFRD project aims to improve the livelihoods of rural poor in project provinces by implementing forest policy reform actions and policies. The four components are: (i) improved planning and management of NPAs and corridors that sustain biodiversity; (ii) improved land use planning and management; (iii) improved forest law enforcement; and (iv) project management. Specific GCF Project 1 Activities and Actions build upon the SFRD approach to improve the capacity for sustainable forest management in the project provinces. The main challenge is to ensure sufficient funding given budget constraints. While the use of new tools was initiated (e.g., a platform for community engagement), there are still challenges with respect to their implementation, including the need for additional resources and institutional support systems. The SFRD project will continue to provide technical assistance in forest law enforcement and ensure that the use of these management tools become a routine activity. A list of activities under Actions 1.4 (Strengthening forest law enforcement) have been informed by these SFRD findings. Activity 3 builds on the substantial experience generated by the SFRD project in activity involving actions in forests

### B.2 The change of target

#### B.2.1 Barriers

The Lao government is fully committed to implementing the Laos ERP and the broader National REDD+ Strategy, there are barriers that need to be addressed with GCF support to ensure the success of the proposed initiatives. These are summarized in Annex 2 (of the feasibility study) (hereinafter). In summary, the barriers in the project area can be categorized as follows:

- **Lack of long-term sustainable financing for forest management and enforcement:** Government budgets are concentrated on short-term projects and do not provide sufficient funding to cover the costs associated with forest sector fees and taxes (e.g., income tax, timber harvesting taxes, land taxes) nor land. Where there is potential revenue from forest products, such as non-timber forest products (NTFPs), payments for ecosystem services linked to hydropower schemes and road tolls associated with infrastructure development, such measures have not been formalized or legislated at all.

- **Gaps in the legal framework:** REDD+ is not fully mainstreamed into Socio-Economic Development Plans (SEDPs), and gaps and inconsistencies in forest legislation limit the effectiveness of forest governance. The Lao government has not yet adopted a National Forest Policy, nor has it explicitly made an exception for exceptional sustainable forest management; and Prime Minister Order 9 (2018) includes incentives and penalties for smaller engagement in unsustainable agriculture.

- **Insufficient land use planning and coordination:** Land-use planning is contributing to deforestation through unclear land use rules, inconsistent or absent boundary demarcation, and lack of enforcement. The Lao government has not yet adopted a National Forest Policy, and forest land use planning processes pertain to agricultural land and forest land resulting in disconnected strategies and monitoring.

- **Insufficient law enforcement:** There is currently weak policy coherence and cross-sectoral coordination; policies continue to place emphasis on land-use activities that support economic growth (e.g. cash crop cultivation, mining) rather than sustainable forest management. The Lao government has not yet adopted a National Forest Policy, and law enforcement agencies such as the forest inspection are under-staffed and under-equipped, lacking up-to-date maps and GPS equipment for example; and even lacking budgets and vehicles to travel to the field.

An overview of the legal and regulatory framework governing the forest sector in Lao PDR is provided in Annex 2a. A review of the Agro-Biodiversity Initiative commissioned by the Swiss Agency for Development and Cooperation.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL (2.0) PAGE 15 OF 95

B

* **Insufficient practical experience and skills for implementing good agricultural practices:** Land use practices commonly applied in the Project 1 area are characterized by low productivity and cannot typically be considered good agricultural practice. These include shifting cultivation, which is a form of subsistence agriculture (upland rice, vegetables, etc.) and certain cash crops (e.g., maize, Job’s tears and cassava) to less suitable areas such as uplands and forest lands. This has led to deforestation, soil erosion, loss of biodiversity and producers seeking out more productive land, which is leading to further deforestation and forest degradation. The lack of good agricultural practices also limits the productivity of agricultural production, as does the limited availability of government agricultural extension services.

* **Insufficient practical experience and skills for implementing sustainable forest management:** Weak policy coherence and enforcement of laws and regulations related to forest management and land use planning, combined with poor institutional capacity in the forestry sector. This has led to illegal clearing and degradation of forested lands driven by inappropriate agricultural practices, insufficient land-use planning and weak law enforcement (see above barriers).

* **Limited access to finance for local villagers and small and medium enterprises:** Poverty and lack of alternative livelihoods are major constraints on the ability of households in the project area to generate sufficient income. This limits household income in the project area. Nonetheless, there is a notable absence of financing opportunities for villagers and SMEs to invest in productive assets or expand their existing businesses into other economic activities because: (i) many Lao financial institutions have poor penetration in rural areas, making it physically difficult for them to provide loans; (ii) interest rates on loans are high compared with the income earned (typically around 10% in annualised terms) and high and act to discourage lending; (iii) financial institutions perceive that SMEs and villagers lack the capacity to prepare business plans or provide proper accounting, thereby discouraging financial institutions from lending to them.

B.3 Project description

B.3.1 Structure of Project 1

Output 1: Enabling environment for REDD+ implementation

56. Objective 1.1: Enhancing the enabling environment for REDD+ implementation through three principal means: enhancing the financing environment for forest sector transformation, revising and strengthening the legal framework for Lao PDR’s forestry, and improving enforcement of new regulatory measures.

### 7. Activity 1.1.1: Establishing a Lao PDR Forest Fund

The establishment of a Forest Fund is seen as a critical enabling measure to unlock the hitherto unexploited potential for Lao PDR to raise substantial long-term climate finance. Project 1 is specifically designed to help Lao PDR establish such a fund, which will be used to channel resources from various sources (e.g., carbon credits, alternative REDD+ results-based payments) into specific projects that provide multiple co-benefits for the Lao forestry and agriculture sectors. In this context, establishment of a Lao PDR Forest Fund is seen as an important enabling measure to unlock the hitherto unexploited potential for Lao PDR to raise substantial long-term climate finance; for financing are earmarked for specific REDD+ sectoral actions, for channeling funds to intended beneficiaries, and for providing a platform for multi-stakeholder coordination.

Two existing Lao funds, the Forest and Forest Resource Development Fund (FFRDF) and the Environmental Protection Fund (EPF), are good candidates to host the REDD+ Funding Window. The FFRDF (under the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry) is a trust fund established in 2013 by the Government for the management of forest and village lands. It funds forestry activities, including conservation and protection of watersheds and protected areas. The EPF (under the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment) was established in 2007 to manage and disburse significant amounts of financing that meet international fiduciary standards (see the Capacity Needs Assessment). Since its establishment, the EPF has been used for a wide range of activities related to forest management. The EPF has initiated capacity development of the FFRDF and channeled small grants from the FFRDF to village groups located in protected areas. The EPF also provides technical assistance to the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment (MoNRE) that has a less-forgiving mandate for financial environmental protection, sustainable natural resource management, and climate change adaptation. The EPF is currently receiving technical support from a GEF-managed project (the Lao Second Land Eacironment and Social [LENS2] project). The EPF is currently receiving technical support from a GEF-managed project (the Lao Second Land Eacironment and Social [LENS2] project).

The FFRDF has a natural legal and sectoral mandate to operate the REDD+ Funding Window but is currently unable to exercise this capability, whereas the EPF has a broader environmental mandate but a proven track-record in managing financial resources. The EPF is also more likely to be able to provide the necessary technical assistance and through-organizational participation in the REDD+ Funding Window. If and when the FFRDF becomes capable of operating the REDD+ Funding Window, it will gradually assume responsibility for this function and assume progressively more responsibility until eventually the entire REDD+ Funding Window is transferred from the EPF to the FFRDF. The alternative scenario under consideration by the Government involves an eventual merger of the EPF and FFRDF.

Under the REDD+ Funding Window, it is anticipated that the FFRDF will receive grant resources from the EPF and, in turn, channel these funds into specific projects that provide multiple co-benefits for intended beneficiaries. The EPF will continue to manage its own trust fund and other such grant payments.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FINANCE PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 18 OF 95

B

villages where such systems are in place. Community-managed financial institutions, such as Village Development Funds (VDFs), offer considerable potential for outreach and financial inclusion. If well managed, they can also provide a platform to support the development of other services, including microfinance.

Accordingly, each of the Project 1 target villages will, upon request, be supported in starting and operating a Village Development Fund (VDF). The VDFs will be established as independent legal entities with their own board. Project 1 will build on this work by assessing the current situation in terms of VDFs in 200 villages (and subsequently scaling up to all 350 villages) and identifying those that are well-functioning, those that have some level of functionality but need support, and those that do not yet exist. This assessment will be based on a combination of quantitative data (e.g., the number of VDFs in each village), qualitative information about their functioning, and the extent to which they can be expected to support existing EVDIs and to identify suitable approaches to channel funding to villages. This assessment and corresponding recommendations for action will be presented as part of the Project 1 Mid-term Review.

Project 1 will strengthen the capacities of VDFs and will provide financial support to expand into target villages not yet covered by VDFs.


As in many low-income countries, individuals and small and medium-size enterprises (SMEs) agribusinesses in Lao PDR typically face multiple constraints on growth. A key barrier is access to finance and, specifically, limited lending from commercial banks. This limits the ability of SMEs to invest in productive assets, expand their operations, and the ability to prepare business plans. This makes banks hesitant to lend to these SMEs, for both poor management practices and lack of collateral. In addition, there are no formal credit bureaus or rating systems in the country; many financial institutions do not have branches in district capitals, let alone in rural areas. Moreover, high interest rates (15-20%) on loans from commercial banks make it difficult to access finance for SMEs. This is due to a low level of commercialisation of the agricultural sector, and the lack of enterprises' access to external markets.

Activity 1.1 consists of 4 Actions:

### Action 1.1.1: Mobilizing sustainable forest sector financing

Project 1 will assist the government in developing a comprehensive strategy for mobilizing resources from all relevant sectors (public and private) for the forest sector production, private sector activity and financing streams. The assessment will include recommendations for structuring the forest sector financing mechanism to ensure that it is efficient and effective. It will also assess potential new domestic sources such as payments for ecosystem services (PES) from the hydropower industry, which could be used to finance forest conservation activities. This will be done in close collaboration with private sector finance from, for example, impact investors seeking robust bottom-line social and financial returns on their investments.

The strategy will be developed by the Department of Planning and Finance under MOPR together with the responsible government entities (notably, the Department of Planning and Finance under MOPR) and the World Bank.

### Action 1.1.2: Enabling the EFF to FFRDFs to establish and operate the REDD+ Funding Window

The EFF will receive GCF funds for a number of defined Project 1 activities – see below. As part of this, the EFF will be supported in establishing and operating a new funding window within its Project Management Unit (PMU). The EFF will, through the REDD+ Funding Window, then disburse and monitor these funds to support the establishment and operation of Village Development Funds (VDFs) via the FRDRF, CSOs and SMEs. The EFF's roles and responsibilities in this regard are described below.

The Project Operational Management Plan (POM) will be developed within the first few months of Project commencement, with a focus on how to ensure that the funding window is effectively established and operated together with the REDD+ Funding Window.


The EFF will also work in redesigning its governance structure and developing standard operating procedures (SOPs), manuals and internal guidance documents that meet international standards. The new funding window will be supported by an Information Technology (IT) system to allow FRDRF to operate professionally and to undertake financial management on an electronic basis.

FRDRF will also receive training in project management, financial management, and capacity development support to build the needed skills for FRDRF. FFRDF will provide technical assistance as required. The EFF will be responsible for ensuring that all relevant stakeholders are considered to provide them with on-the-job training.


The EFF, through the REDD+ Funding Window, will transfer small grants to the FRDRF and slowly increase its funding over time. The FRDRF will use these funds to support the establishment of VDFs based grant for activities in target villages in the framework of Activities 21.1-3.1-3.3 of Project 1, and in compliance with the eligible activities of FFRDF Decree No 50 (2003).

### Activity 1.2: Mainstreaming REDD+ into the NDC and socio-economic development plans (SEDPs) (2021-2025)

At the provincial level, SEDPs are prepared in 5-year plans and are the primary strategy reference for governmental entities to fulfill their work. SEPDs and sectoral plans provide the basis for government budgeting, and thus it is vital that REDD+ be mainstreamed into these documents.

o **Activity 1.2.1:** Mainstreaming of REDD+ into the National Development Contribution (NDC). Provision of technical and logistical support to government staff to integrate the updated NDC with the new REDD+ strategy, which will be submitted in 2023. The draft NDC (September 2022) contains a prominent forestry and REDD+ component but does not explicitly reference REDD+.

o **Activity 1.2.2:** Mainstreaming of REDD+ into provincial socio-economic development plans (SEDPs) (2021-2025). Provision of technical and logistical support to government staff for the integration of Provincial REDD+ Action Plans with SEDPs, which are prepared in 5-year plans. The draft SEDPs were developed in 2017/2018* and inform the provincial SEDP planning horizon.

o **Activity 1.2.3:** Mainstreaming of REDD+ into district socio-economic development plans (SEDPs) (2021-2025). Provision of technical and logistical support to government staff for the integration of Provincial REDD+ Action Plans with SEDPs, which are prepared in 5-year plans.

### Activity 1.3: Strengthening the regulatory framework

The Forest Law (No.6/NA, 2001), like the Land Law, is not harmonized with the provisions of the Forest Policy and the Forestry Law. Meaning that extensive updating of regulations will be needed following revisions to the Law. Although this represents a considerable challenge in terms of implementation, it is necessary for ensuring consistency (which has been internally inconsistent (contradictory in some respects), complex and not very local for authorities to understand). Support will be provided to government staff to ensure the development of an enabling environment that incentivizes investments that conform with land use plans (including deforestation-free commitments) and forest management plans, while also ensuring compliance with laws and regulations related with local communities. Financially executed by BMZ and the Government of Laos PDR (i.e., not requiring GCF financial support).

- **Activity 1.3.1:** Creating an enabling environment for SPM and private sector investment in village-based agroforestry development. Revision, modification and drafting of relevant decrees and regulations that incentivize investments in agroforestry activities by smallholders and other stakeholders (e.g., cooperatives), which act responsibly in the context of local regulatory frameworks and which sustain positive relations with local communities. This will be done through a participatory process involving government staff, BMZ, FLR and village forest.

- **Activity 1.3.2:** Financial and regulatory incentives and barriers for smallholders to improve smallholder engagement in village-based agroforestry. Based on the assessment, Project 1 will support the revision of relevant decrees and regulations that incentivize investments in agroforestry activities by smallholders and other stakeholders (e.g., cooperatives), which act responsibly in the context of local regulatory frameworks and which sustain positive relations with local communities.

## Activity 1.3: Capacity building and awareness-raising on the forest sector legal framework Provision of training to key stakeholders (including CSOs, local government officials, community leaders, village representatives, and broader stakeholder groups) under the key regulations related to forest management and environmental protection.

### Activity 1.4: Law enforcement and monitoring Strengthened law enforcement is a key measure needed to support Lao PDR's commitment to REDD+, as well as other high-level initiatives including PMD 5 and the FLEGT-VA. Legal enforcement is also required for the implementation of forest management plans, which are subject to provincial authorities to address due to limited budgets, limited technical capacities and understanding, but also to a party unclear on their roles.

A number of donor projects have supported improved law enforcement in Laos PDR. The GIZ CIFOR project, in cooperation with the Wildlife Conservation Society (WCS) and counterparts, developed a provincial Law Enforcement Action Plan (LEAP) for Forests in Huay Xai Province. The LEAP includes five key elements: (i) strengthening coordination, communication, and exchange mechanisms; (ii) strengthening law enforcement capacity through training and equipment; (iii) increasing the use of technology and tactics; (iv) increasing capacity for law enforcement through training; (v) upgrading enforcement policy and procedures.

The SDFOR project in Luang Namtha Province has supported the development of a LEAP for the SuorFORD project in four provinces (Sayaboury, Luang Namtha, Bolikhamxay and Oudomxay), where law enforcement staff are trained on forest management plans. The project also supports the implementation of the Specialized Tactical Enforcement Patrol Programme (STEPPI) and the Spatial Monitoring and Reporting Tool (SMART®). The SMART tool is a web-based software that allows for the collection, analysis, and reporting of spatial data in near real-time. It has been used to monitor forest fires, illegal logging, and other activities.

The SDFOR project also supports the implementation of the Spatial Patrol Information and Response Tracking (SPRIT) system in conjunction with the Department of Forest Inspection (DOI), which facilitates the collection and analysis of patrol information. The SPRIT system is a web-based software that allows for the collection, analysis, and reporting of spatial data in near real-time.

Further support has been initiated to ensure that the use of these management tools becomes more widespread across Laos PDR.

### Activity 1.4 consists of 7 Actions:

- Review of standard operating procedures (SOPs) for Forestry Law Enforcement that are aligned with the existing legal framework.

- Implementation of a LEAP in key provinces (e.g., Sayaboury, Luang Namtha, Bolikhamxay, Oudomxay) consisting of key actors (DFOs, PAFs, DOF, PDNRE, PDNRE, P-WEN [Provincial Wildlife Enforcement Network], and other relevant stakeholders).
- Establishment of a LEAP in the provinces of Luang Prabang, Xieng Khouang, and Bokeo.
- A number of CSOs are interested in the development of a mechanism for management of non-commercial timber from Village Use Forests, with the support of the P-WEN. The P-WEN has been established to provide technical assistance to the villages and to facilitate the implementation of the network. The network has received funding from USAID and the CGI to conduct research on the types of village use forests in order to promote their sustainable management.
- A *khor* is a cluster of villages.

### 61. Activity 1.5: Land use planning and improved tenure security

In order to improve tenure security, the government has put land registration and land titling high on its agenda. The government's principal objective is to secure the land tenure rights of smallholder farmers in Lao PDR, particularly those who are landless or have insecure tenure over their respective title lands. Land tenure security has a direct correlation with the food security of smallholders and agricultural productivity. It also provides them with greater certainty about the future use of their land for investment in land. However, the associated land use planning has typically been developed with a focus on a specific sector (e.g., agriculture or forestry) rather than as an integrated system-wide approach to land management. Moreover, local land use plans are not well synchronised with higher-level plans that may take a more integrated and broader perspective.

Land use planning in Lao PDR builds on over 20 years of experience of donor-funded projects (e.g., KWF, GIZ, German Agriculture, SIDA, TADB, AgriSud) as well as national initiatives to conduct land use planning. Such initiatives have been implemented at various scales and levels of detail, often in collaboration with representatives of the villages and neighboring villages), or internal zoning of these village territories into forest, agricultural, and residential zones, which are then allocated among the members of the village or village households). Together, these elements form the basis of Participatory Land Use Plans (PLUPs).

Subsequent implementation and monitoring of the PLUPs have typically not formed part of these donor initiatives, however. The Government of Lao PDR has recently launched a new initiative called the National Land Project. This project will use these existing PLUPs and will support their implementation (including augmenting them with short-term capacity building) in order to provide a more integrated approach to land management at the level of Land and Water – using government co-finance – develop and implement a programme of systematic land regulation. Activity 1.5 contributes directly towards this objective.

### Action 1.5-1: Maintaining FR guiding principles into land use planning manuals and guidelines

* **Revision:** Revision of existing manuals and guidelines relating to land use planning in order to integrate a reference to the Forest Resource Management Law (FRML) and its implementation, including forest landscape restoration through a range of forestry and non-forestry sector actions.
* **Dissemination:** Dissemination of revised manuals and guidelines.
* **Awareness-raising:** Capacity building on the revised manuals and guidelines.

### Action 1.5-2: Participatory village land use planning (PLUP) in target project districts (linked with Activity 1.4 and Outputs 2 and 3 in hotspot areas)

The ERP is a program that aims at reducing GHG emissions in accordance with the Kyoto Protocol's Annex I Level (L1). - In 2020/2025, the fourth national forest inventory will be undertaken, following the methods and Design of the third national forest inventory. The design involves the establishment of 720 plots within different forest types and 120 plots within regenerating vegetation. The Lao PDR National Forest Inventory (LNF) is a comprehensive land cover survey that provides information on land use, which is fully consistent with the methodology used in the development of the Emission Reductions Program (ERP). This will be done to ensure consistency between the national forest inventory and the estimation of GHG benefits of Project 1 (including leakage effects), as well as supporting other REDD+ initiatives such as the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF) and the Climate, Community & Biodiversity Standard (CCB).

## Activity 16.8.3 Calculation of emission and removal factors Based on the data from the forest inventory, the emission/removal factors for the different forest and non-forest classes will be revised and modified, reflecting the new land use/land cover development work that has focused on the reported carbon pools: above-ground biomass and below-ground biomass.

## Activity 16.8.4 Assessment of leakage effects This Action will specifically assess the GHG emissions and removals associated with forest degradation and deforestation in the project area. The assessment will be based on a comparison between the current situation and what would have increased/decreased within the project area compared with other parts of the country. This Action will also contribute to the development of a methodology for assessing leakage effects, which will include knowledge international (cross-border) displacement of unsustainable forestry practices in the Lower Mekong region.

## Activity 16.8.5 Reporting on reduced GHG emissions and enhanced carbon stocks The reporting of achieved GCF and UNFCCC reporting requirements. This Action will ensure that Lao PDR’s REDD+ data is consistently used for GCF reporting, for FCPF Carbon Fund reporting to help achieve the country’s climate change mitigation goals. It will also support the development of National Communication (NCs) in the UNFCCC. The reporting will be subject to a technical assessment by the FCPF and UNFCCC.

Design and set-up of a carbon registry.

Capacity building for data collection to address new requirements.

Capacity building for localisation of system operation and maintenance.

## Activity 2.3: Sustainable rural infrastructure and watershed management

### Background
The project is designed to address the challenges of low productivity, limited access to markets, poor water quality, and lack of livelihood opportunities in smallholder farming communities in eleven small-scale irrigation scheme upgrades (all of them in the geographical scope of Project 1) to secure farmer access to markets and reduce poverty. The project will also aim at reducing pressure from over-exploitation of forest resources on land use change, which may remain intact through improved land management (including good agricultural practices) and reduced pressures from other activities such as livestock grazing.

### Objectives
The project aims to improve the livelihoods of smallholder farmers in the eleven small-scale irrigation schemes by increasing their income generation from high-value crops and livestock. Farmers will also benefit from increased access to markets, improved water quality, and better land use management through improved agricultural practices and reduced pressure on forest resources.

### Expected Outcomes
- Improved livelihoods for smallholder farmers.
- Increased market access for high-value crops and livestock.
- Enhanced water quality in the project area.
- Reduced pressure on forest resources from over-exploitation.

## Action 2.3.3: Nutrient-sensitive agriculture is targeted communities

* The Action will strengthen the nutritional status of children under five years old.
    * District Nutrition Teams (DNTs) will be formed in each of the target districts, consisting of 4 members from each district.
        - DNTs will be responsible for working with 10-15 villages.
    * Village Community Nutrition Committees (VCNCs) will provide support follow-up with households on progress and challenges. VCNCs and communities will discuss key nutrition indicators, such as the number and diversity of foods consumed by children under five years old, the frequency of feeding, hygiene concepts, the percentage of children with malnutrition and the dietary delay for different household groups.

## Output 3: Climate change mitigation action through forestry

### 68. Output 3 will build upon Output 1 (ensuring environment) and will reduce emissions through sustainable forest landscape management and the promotion of forest landscape restoration (FLR), especially on degraded lands. Activities under this output are expected to be completed by 2025.

* **Village forests** are forests located within a village area, which the government has allocated to the village to manage, preserve and use in a sustainable manner in accordance with the legal and regulatory framework.
    * Village Forest Management Plans (VFMPs) will be developed for each village forest. The VFMP is expected to be completed by 2019.

* **Production Forest Areas** (PFAs) are forest and forestland areas allocated to the State for management and which have been declared as PFAs under the National Forestry Act No. 38/2004, or under the Management of Natural Timber under the management of the Department of Forestry (in MAF), although PFA 31 has imposed a ban on such harvesting since 2013.

    * EU and FAD funding will also support the implementation of this Action. No GOFCF financial support is required.
        - For further information about REEDD+ implementation at the village forest level, see GEF (2018), Forest Governance, REEDD+ and Village Forest Management Plans, in: GEF 6th Cycle Project Document, Forest Legacy Compendium. Department of Forestry Inspection and Department of Forestry, Lao PDR, p.342. Provided in Annex Zv.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL (2.0) PAGE 38 OF 95

## Action 3.1: Village forest management plans (VFMPs)

### 3.1.1 Development of village forest management plans (VFMPs)
As with the development of the VFMPs, the implementation and monitoring of VFMPs will follow the procedures and procedures outlined in the Forest Management Guidelines.

#### Stage 1: Demarcation of forest areas and preparation of detailed village forest map
- Detailed village forest maps will be prepared by the Department of Forestry using aerial photographs and satellite imagery.
- The maps will show all forest resources, including trees, shrubs, grasses, and other vegetation types. They will also indicate the location of roads, rivers, streams, and other features that may affect the management of the forest.

#### Stage 2: Preparation of baseline inventory
- A baseline inventory will be prepared for each village forest area.
- The inventory will include information on tree species, diameter at breast height (DBH), age class, health status, and regeneration potential. It will also provide data on soil type, land use, and other relevant factors.

#### Stage 3: Preparation of five-year village forest management plan (VFMP)
- A five-year VFMP will be prepared for each village forest area.
- The plan will outline the objectives, strategies, and actions to be taken over a period of five years. It will include details on tree planting, seedling production, regeneration, silviculture, harvesting, marketing, and other activities.

#### Stage 4: Village forest management agreement
- A management agreement will be signed between the Department of Forestry and each village.
- The agreement will outline the responsibilities and obligations of both parties in implementing the VFMP. It will also provide a framework for resolving any disputes that may arise during the implementation process.

### Action 3.1.2 Implementation and monitoring of VFMPs
As with the development of the VFMPs, the implementation and monitoring of VFMPs will follow the procedures outlined in the Forest Management Guidelines.

## B

### Appendix 6: Implementation of the village forest management activities, following the annual plan of operation and financed by annually-approved grants.

#### Stage 4: Village Forest Management Activities

Resources will be transferred to village development funds (VDFs) using mechanisms similar to those used in the Integrated Conservation of Biodiversity and Forests (ICBF) project. In summary, the ERF and NPMU will increase their annual budget for VDFs by US$ 10 million. The VDFs will be managed according to the criteria. Approved requests will be administered by the FROF. More details on transfer modalities are available in the ICBF project document.

#### Activity 3.2: Sustainable management of production forests

There are 51 production forest areas (PFAs) in Lao PDR. The scale of degradation in PFAs is intense; only 15% of the total area in production forest (465,000 hectares) remains in a natural state (i.e., with no human intervention except for selective logging and harvesting timber). In the first Project 1 program, this forest category covers a total of 700,000 ha. Regenerative vegetation is planted on land that has been degraded and agricultural land of about 32,000 ha in the Project 1 area, which could be used for restoration and reforestation.

There is a ban on timber harvesting within production forest areas in response to widespread unsustainable harvesting practices. The ban was introduced as part of Project 1 since 2005. This has resulted in a major drop of deforestation due to the absence of marketable tree species with minimum harvestable diameters – largely due to the lack of demand for these species and their low value. However, this ban has also led to an increase in forest degradation and need of natural regeneration and sustainable management. While the ban on timber harvesting has had positive impacts on biodiversity conservation and reduced deforestation, it has not been effective in managing production forests. Provincial and district authorities have noted that budgets for government authorities, including those responsible for forest management, are insufficient to ensure the sustainability of production forestry.

In 3 districts in the Project 1 area (Savannakhet Province – Phouk, Sayaboury and Hongsa districts), there is considerable potential for sustainable harvesting and the project will work with the government on a renewed regulation that allows harvesting if the forest management entity can demonstrate long-term sustainability. Activity 3.2 continues.

#### Action 3.2.1: Forest inventory and forest management planning in production forests

- **Designation of villages, mobilization and organization of villagers**: WFM will present the project concept note to the provincial and district authorities for approval. The Village Management Committees (VMCs) led by a district villages forest council (DVFC).
- **Preparation of Forest Management Plans (FMPs)**: participatory land use zoning, marking boundaries and taking an inventory of trees and NTFPs.
The focus of the management planning will be to identify priority areas for sustainable harvesting. The FMPs will be linked to Activity 2.2: the establishment of a public-private dialogue to promote private sector participation in production forestry.
- **Definition of degraded forest and forest access rules (rules governing forest access and benefit sharing arrangements)**: WFM will develop guidelines on the definition of degraded forests, forest access rules, assessment and identification of eligible and promising village-level development investments based on SFM criteria, and forest access rules.
- **Capacity building for VLFPMs and managing inclusive DVFCs**: and training for villagers on SFM, forest management planning and benefit sharing arrangements.
- **Capacity building for provincial and district officials to support forest management planning and eventual implementation of FMPs**.

#### Action 3.2.2: Implementation of Forest Management Plans (FMPs) with associated monitoring
- **Support VLFPMs and DVFCs in the preparation of annual work plans and budget plans to implement forest management under their Forest Management Plans**: including village-based forestry development projects in degraded forests.
- **Providing grants by the REDD+ Funding Window for the implementation of FMPs**.
- **Grants to VLFPMs to support the implementation of annual FMPs**.

### B

- Grants to support forest establishment using the same modalities as for Action 1.2 (50% matching grant with a private sector investor).
- Village Forest Management Committees (VFCs) will be established.

### Activity 3.3: National Protected Area Management

The Project area contains 3 National Protected Areas (NPAs) - Nam Sam and Nam Ei Phou Louey in Houaphan province and Nam Poy in Sayaboury province. They span 12,000 hectares of forested land, with an additional 48,600 hectares of adjacent areas under community management agreements covering approximately 670,000 ha in the Project area. Approximately 95% of this protected area is classified as forest.

A lack of management plans and availability of financing are major barriers to addressing deforestation and forest degradation in these NPAs. Activity 3.3 aims to build on the extensive experience generated by the KNF Integrated Forest Management Programme (IFMP) and other projects, including the Nam Sam NPA. Pre-feasibility and feasibility studies were conducted that informed the design of the project (2012-2015). The project will be implemented in collaboration with the Ministry of Environment and other partners based on experiences from Nam Ei Phou Louey NPA (Wildlife Conservation Society – WCS®; World Bank®) and Nam Xam NPA (WCS®; USAID®). In the context of WCS, close exchange and cooperation is envisaged regarding the Nam Ei Phou Louey NPA.

Villages will be able to access village-based grants for a set of eligible agricultural and forestry activities that support forest conservation and provide income generation. These activities are based on the Village Fund Disbursement (Management Guidelines), which permit activities such as the following:

- Improved forest use through bamboo farming for income generation.
- Conservation of non-timber forest products.
- Non-timber forest products for income generation.
- Agroforestry.

These grants will reduce the pressure on deforestation and release of unsustainable forest use, and will improve household incomes. The application process is described in the Project Implementation Manual (Annex 22e).

### Activity 3.3.1: Development or revision of National Protected Area (NPA) management plans

The development of NPA management plans and revision of existing (outdated) plans in the Project area will help strengthen the governance structure, improve forest management practices, and provide a basis for the livelihoods of villagers living within or adjacent to the NPAs. This Action will include the following measures:

- Information obtained from MAF and MAHRE representatives, PRAIP consultations, ADGF 2015.
- This project supports the effective management of 2 large landscapes containing NPA and continues contributing to sustainable biodiversity conservation in these areas by providing a basis for improved forest governance and livelihoods.
- More information on WCF activities within this protected area are available online: http://www.pncb.org/Projects/NamEiPhouLouey
- The project will be implemented with the support of the Ministry of Environment, Wildlife Conservation Society (WCS), World Bank, USAID, and other partners.
- Strengthening the capacity and management of the Nam Ei Phou Louey National Protected Area in the face of expanding infrastructure development.

### 8.4.2 Project Management, Implementation, Monitoring and Evaluation

#### Role of GIZ as Accredited Entity and Executing Entity

GIZ head office (in Germany) will serve as the Accredited Entity (AE). The GIZ office in Lao PDR will fulfill the role of an Executing Entity (EE) of Project 1, together with the Government of Lao PDR (represented by MAF and JICA). The roles of these two GIZ cells will be strictly separated and they are accountable to different management structures within GIZ.

#### GIZ as Accredited Entity

As the Accredited Entity (AE), GIZ will assume overall responsibility for Project 1, including project preparation and implementation. Responsibilities include:

- Overall responsibility for, and oversight of, Project 1, including project preparation and implementation
- Continuous communication with the AE
- Receiving, reviewing, approving, disbursing, administering and processing the funds (financial management)
- Ensuring proper use of the GCF proceeds
- Assessing the integrity and capacity of the EEs
- Selecting and monitoring the EEs
- Monitoring the subsidiary agreements and the performance of EEs
- Ensuring compliance with the GCF rules and policies
- Maintaining adequate documentation and reporting to the GCF
- Ensuring project risk management
- Ensuring continuous project risk assessment
- Ensuring quality assurance (pre-final and final reviews)

In order to implement Project 1, GIZ will need to establish legal arrangements with the Executing Entities (the Government of Lao PDR and JICA), Implementation Partners and Contributing Partners.

### For Project 1, the following agreements will be necessary:

- BMZ Commission
- Funding Agreement (FAA) between GIZ as the Accredited Entity and the GCF
- Lao PDR-Germany bilateral agreement with the Ministry of Planning and Investment (MPI)
- Executive Agreement

A subsidiary agreement (grant agreement) between GIZ Head Office (AE) and the Government of Lao PDR will be needed to cover the activities in the REDD+ Project. This agreement will also include co-financing among other things, and address the implementation of those activities co-financed by IFAD, KfW and ADB.
