## 83. GIZ will establish a National Project Management Unit (NPMU)

The NPMU will be established within the Department of Forestry (Df/MAF), associated provincial PMUs (PPMUs) and district PMUs (DPMUs). The NPMU will work in close cooperation with the Df, MAF, PPMUs and DPMUs. It will also liaise with other offices (PAFOs) and DPMUs in District Agriculture and Forestry Offices (DAFOs). The NPMU will have the following responsibilities under this project:

* Providing guidance with respect to developing annual work plans and budgets.
* The NPMU, and – by extension, GIZ as the executing entity – will be responsible for approving work plans and budgets. Approval of work plan budgets will be the responsibility of the NPMU. The NPMU - and, by extension, GIZ as the executing entity - will also be responsible for developing annual indicative criteria to be developed and will determine the appropriateness of each proposed plan (preliminary indicative criteria are provided in Annex 1). The NPMU will also be responsible for approving work plans and budgets. It will conduct appropriate due diligence of the recipient entity by the EPF supported – if necessary - by the NPMU and PPMUs/DPMUs. In case of any concerns, the NPMU will request the EPF to disclose funds according to the plan, provided there are no fiduciary concerns.
* The NPMU will also have a role in giving guidance to the EPF on monitoring the implementation of the work plans.
* There is also guidance given to the EPF in terms of fiduciary responsibility, monitoring and reporting, and compliance with safeguards.

## 84. GIZ as an Executing Entity will be responsible for a GCF budget for Project I of Euro 4.1 million.

NPMU, PPMUs and DPMUs

A National Project Management Unit (NPMU) will be established within the Department of Forestry (Df)/MAF. This structure is similar to that in DF/EPF as a fostered joint ownership of Project I: build institutional capacity and to ensure continuous linkage with Government policy-making and implementation of other initiatives. As an Executing Entity, GIZ will be responsible for the overall management of the project, including the establishment of the NPMU, as well as the establishment and management of Provincial Project Management Units (PPMUs) in case of any provincial PMUs that are established within the framework of the DF/EPF. The same structure will be used for the implementation of GIZ-managed BMZ financing and other projects. The NPMU will provide overall guidance to PPMUs/DPMUs on project-related activities and will provide technical and financial management support. In case to ensure capacity remains in Df/MAF after completion of Project I, the NPMU will also be responsible for ensuring that the institutional capacity generated from sustainable forestry during the course of the project. After project completion, Df will be prepared by making use of the lessons learned, experiences, generate revenues and manage them through the Forest and Forest Resource Development Fund.

PPMUs and DPMUs under the instruction of the NPMU, will assist the NPMU in executing the above responsibilities. PPMUs/DPMUs will also be responsible for ensuring that the project beneficiaries are trained from an additional responsibility at the provincial and district levels. In the event that project beneficiaries (e.g., Village Development Committees) are not able to prepare their own annual operational plans and budgets, PPMUs and DPMUs will assist beneficiaries to complete appropriate plans.

RED+ Funding Window project cycle and fund disbursement process

The EFF will be cleared funds under the RED+ Funding Window. A detailed capacity assessment of the EFF is provided in Annex 23D and a due diligence assessment is provided in Annex 20a. The EFF was established in June 2014 as part of the South African Government’s commitment to support sustainable forest management, biodiversity conservation and community development in the country (under the Environmental Protection and Biodiversity Conservation Act No. 50 of 2004) and is funded by the World Bank under the South Africa Project Delivery Agencies (SDA) from the World Bank Second Law Environment and Social (L&ES) project[1]. The EFF has been established in accordance with the requirements of the World Bank’s Forest Investment (FIC) financing windows, as well as building on the EFF’s capacity and support for project administration and implementation.

There are three considerations underlying the EFF’s role operating the RED+ Funding Window. First, it is desirable that a local, capable entity is involved in project financing in order to increase country-ownership of activities under the RED+ Fund. Second, the EFF has been established as part of the South African Government and through a UNDP-implemented GEF project[2]. Section 1 projects aim to increase the capacity of government institutions to manage forests sustainably and to support the implementation of the National Forest Programme in project execution, and the Government and GIZ anticipate that local capacity will increase significantly, facilitating amongst others, the EFF’s role. Third, the EFF is established as a separate entity from the SDA, with its own mandate aligned with the goals of the GEF project[3]. The EFF is operationally and legally capable of managing the RED+ Funding Window in accordance with the terms of reference for the EFF and the requirements of the World Bank’s Forest Investment (FIC) financing windows. The EFF will be responsible for disbursing funds from concessions, direct grants from national and international organisations, contributions from individuals and other sources to projects under the RED+ Fund, as well as managing the disbursement of funds for ecosystem services, and fines and indemnities.

The RED+ Funding Window will be established and operationalised under Project 1, and will also be utilised as a funding window for the three additional provinces under Projects 2 and 3 (receive future CCF funding approval). Annex 22b provides a description and budget of the RED+ Funding Window.

The RED+ Funding Window will consist of 4 sub-windows, one for each of four different beneficiary groups: government agencies at national, provincial and district levels; villages (Village Development Funds), VDFs); civil society organisations (CSOs) and private sector entities. The criteria for inclusion in the various sub-windows are developed in a Future Project Operational Manual. Nonetheless, it is anticipated that the Funding Window will have the following characteristics:

• Funds support a government policy, strategy or official plan linked to the NDC, a PRAP, the EFF or the GCF Funding Proposal.

• This funding contributes to at least one intermediate outcome indicator that is relevant to the GCF Activity supporting the beneficiary.

• Also, the activity has a clear project scope (i.e. the accounting area of the Emission Reductions Programme).

The beneficiary is related to one of the four RED+ Funding Windows.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 39 OF 95

## Grant modalities channelled through the REDD+ Funding Window will be of three types: operational funds, upfront payments and performance-based payments.

### Operational payments ensure the implementation of planned activities using a pre-defined budget. Upfront payments are advance payments issued to beneficiaries to support defined activities (outlined in a written budget request submitted by the beneficiary to the GCF). Performance-based payments are made as part of the REDD+ Funding Window to support defined activities that have been achieved according to a pre-defined performance metric.

## The choice of payment modality for particular project elements has been made primarily on the basis of:

- Beneficiary capabilities and incentives (e.g. a realistic assessment of their ability to implement activities using available resources).
- Project design and implementation capacity.
- The nature of the activity (e.g., whether it is planned or ongoing).

### While the choice between upfront payments and performance-based payments is somewhat artificial, upfront payments will be issued on an annual basis in advance of project activities being implemented. Performance-based payments are made as part of a funding request. This provides an opportunity for the NPMU to assess beneficiary performance to date and, if needed, to adjust the amount paid out or to withhold subsequent payments. Thus, even for upfront payments there is a performance-element.

## Grand grants to beneficiaries through the REDD+ Funding Window will be between approximately Euro 2,000 and Euro 15 million per project. The size of each grant will be determined by the amount requested by the beneficiary for specific activities (in the form of an ex ante written budget request submitted to the GCF). The maximum grant size is limited to the amount that can be disbursed within the lifetime of the beneficiary’s initial request, and if there are no on-site performance elements – on the verified performance of the implementation team. In cases where the project involves a number of villages or communities (including village levels), workplans will be developed during joint planning meetings and through the GCF’s Project Management Unit (NPMU). The NPMU will then develop a grant request based on how plans, timelines, budgets, concerned organisations, implementing agencies, performance metrics (if relevant), etc., will be managed.

## Through the REDD+ Funding Window, the EPF will channel Euro 11.1 million of the GCF grant to Project 1 beneficiaries. As laid out in the figure below, EPF roles relating to the REDD+ Funding Window will include:

- **Disbursement of funds directly to SMEs, CSOs, the FPRD and province-level (PAPIS, POMRE, POFIs) and district-level (POFs) implementing agencies.** The EPF will not be responsible for disbursing funds to the EPP. The EPP will not, however, have responsibility to approve the technical eligibility of funds to be disbursed. Instead, this is a function of the GCF’s Project Management Unit (NPMU). The NPMU will review or reject disbursement requests based on its fiduciary judgment. In many cases, particularly relating to the FPRD and PAPIS, this may involve reviewing the project implementation in relation to small entities (e.g., village groups) due to their presence in the districts.
- **Providing oversight of the disbursement process and ensuring the responsibility of the EPF.**
- **Monitoring of financial flows under the GIZ-Government of Leo PDR sub-sidiary agreement and ensuring receipt of all required documentation by the NPMU.**
- **Reporting to GIZ as its capacity as AE against project milestones and evaluations will be the responsibility of the FPRD. The EPF will not have any role in this regard, but will ensure that the FPRD is meeting these requirements that are ensuring under according to the GCF’s requirements and will submit monitoring reports to GIZ on a regular basis.**
- **Ensuring compliance against GCF environmental, social and governance safeguards will be the responsibility of the EPF.**

In order to deliver against these responsibilities, the EPF will hire new staff and devote significant resources to managing the project activities. The fees for the EPF to accomplish these responsibilities will be agreed after GCF project approval.

## Coordination between the EPF and the Executing Entities – and, in particular, with GIZ and the Government of Leo PDR – will be ensured through regular coordination meetings. Furthermore, GIZ will steer capacity building initiatives that will ensure that the EPF is able to meet its responsibilities under this agreement. This will address the gaps identified in the GIZ’s capacity assessment (Annex 2). GIZ will assist the EPF to: establish an internal control system for disbursement of funds; develop a financial management plan and budgeting framework, accounting systems, and elaborate and implement manuals, training and advisories to financial staff prior to and during REDD+ Funding Window implementation; improve the IT infrastructure so that it complies with international

## Government sub-window: Government agencies at the national and sub-national levels will be responsible for preparing work plans and associated budget requests to the NPMU in order to resource activities from the REDD+ Funding Window. The NPMU will approve them. The following Project 1 Activities are relevant to this sub-window:

- 1.1: REDD+ Funding Window and sustainable finance
- 1.2: Strengthening forest governance, including improved law enforcement and monitoring
- 1.3: Land use planning and improved tenure security
- 1.4: Forest carbon accounting and verification system
- 1.7: Implementing knowledge management, the ESMF and Gender Action Plan, FPIC and safeguards
- 2.1: Strengthening forest governance at national level
- 2.2: Catalyzing private sector investment in value chains
- 3.1: Sustainable use of forest resources
- 3.2: Sustainable management of production forests
- 3.3: Forest landscape restoration and management
- 4.1: Project management, coordination, monitoring and reporting

## Village sub-window: Villages are a key project beneficiary, as local inhabitants are often most directly involved in determining the success or failure of REDD+ activities. The involvement of village-level entities is critical to the success of the project. Village Development Funds (VDFs) will be the primary entities representing villages and will be responsible for preparing grant proposals under the following Project 1 Activities:

- 2.1: Local incentives for good agricultural practices and agroforestry
- 3.1: Village forest management
- 3.2: Sustainable use of forest resources and production forests
- 3.3: National Protected Area (NPA) management

Village Committees will be required to submit work plans to the EFPF/FFD in order to receive resources for VDFs. The establishment of Village Development Funds is a two-step process – establishment of a VDF committee with 4–6 members, at least 2 of them women, with the right to withdraw funds; activities screened against a 'white list' of eligible projects. At the village level coordination staff will support villages in preparing funding work plans, and the NPMU will approve them.

## B.4.3 Regional taxation and insurance

### Privileges, immunities and insurance

104. The Federal Republic of Germany has concluded Framework Agreements on Technical Cooperation under international public law with the Government of Lao PDR, which provide for certain privileges and immunities to be applied in connection with the implementation of the project. These include exemption from customs duties and fees. GCF will endeavor to reach arrangements to have these privileges and immunities also applied to Project Activities through GCF processes. However, formal agreements will only be entered into after the project has been approved.

#### Approvals, permits, licences and so on

105. At the time of submitting this Funding Proposal, GCF is not aware of the project requiring specific approvals, permissions, licences or so on to allow for the implementation of planned activities.

### Currency

106. The local currency in Lao is the KIP code (LAK). The project's local transactions will use LAK. The AE will manage GCF proceeds in Euro. GCF will apply its standard rates for the conversion of currency.

### Insurance

107. GIZ, as the AE, will ensure that project activities are adequately insured as per GIZ standard operating procedures and common practices. GIZ standard operating procedures require contracts to ensure required insurance cover. GIZ policies provide insurance cover for GCF. At the time of submitting the Funding Proposal, GIZ does not envisage any specific requirements in relation to cover scope related to this new project.

## B.5 Justification for GCF funding request

### 108. After decades of losing forest land, Lao PDR is actively seeking to implement an ambitious REDD+ emission reduction strategy that will require substantial investment over the next decade to achieve its goals with bilateral and multilateral donors, to reach its current level of REDD+ readiness and ability to transform its forest sector. But capacity constraints in the forestry sector are a major barrier to scaling up REDD+. The Government of Lao PDR requests GCF support to remove investment barriers to unlock future results-based payments, domestic taxes and fees, and to create a sustainable financing mechanism for REDD+.

### 109. No other country anywhere in the world has been targeted to achieve sustainable management of its forests and landscapes alone. The degree and complexity of the necessary change is high. The need for financial and technical support is substantial. The project will be implemented through a combination of GCF funding, the implementation of mitigation and adaptation actions (the equivalent of approximately 10% of GDP). For forest sector activities, the Government of Lao PDR has committed to co-finance up to EUR 25 million (USD 34 million).

### 110. Lao PDR needs GCF support that goes beyond the potential of the government or its development partners:

#### More public financing: The Government of Lao PDR has already committed to contributing a substantial amount of co-finance (Euro 11.5 million) to Project 1. The project, specifically Activity 1.1, will itself devise mechanisms for additional public funding.

#### More grants from other development partners: All of Lao PDR's current development partners in the forest sector already support the GCF project. Germany and Japan have both committed grant financing to Project 1 and are expected to provide further financial contributions to support the scaling up of sustainable forest practices and to reduce the pressure on expansion into forested landscapes. The World Bank (IFC) currently provides technical assistance for the development of the ESMF. GCF will work closely with GIZ and the government to support the development of the project by scheduling the ESMF

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FINANCE PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 46 OF 95

## Additional basis for the Government of La PDR (La PDR is a landlocked least developed country (LLDC) that has been identified as a High-Impact Country under the Least Developed Countries (LDCs) Debt Sustainability Analysis®). La PDR's risk of external debt distress remains high, suggesting the urgent need to sign up for fiscal policy reform and strengthen public financial management. The government is pursuing a long-term debt management strategy. Despite these constraints, the government is willing to borrow Euro 12.3 million from the Green Climate Fund (GCF) in support of Project 1. This will be used to finance additional borrowing for Project 1 as well as additional and continued borrowing by the government at the expense of future generations and local communities.

### Loans for households: The beneficiary groups - village foresters and subsistence farmers, are among the poorest population groups in La PDR, with a high dependence on natural resources and poor access to markets. These groups have been identified as particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts. Approximately 28% of inhabitants in the Project area live below the poverty line (1) higher than the national average of 23%. The project will provide an important opportunity for these groups, who are currently without financing instrument for these beneficiary groups. The GCF's added value will be to support the poorest population groups and help them adapt to climate change.

#### 11C. The GCF grant for Project 1 will be used to overcome the government’s constraints to source public funding or assume additional debt to invest in measures needed to reduce deforestation and forest degradation. La PDR is a landlocked least developed country (LLDC) that has been identified as a High-Impact Country under the Least Developed Countries Debt Sustainability Analysis®. La PDR's risk of external debt distress remains high, suggesting the urgent need to sign up for fiscal policy reform and strengthen public financial management.

#### 12C. Project 1 will seek resources to mobilise additional public and private domestic finance to support REDD+. In particular, the project will support and strengthen the capacities of two national financial institutions: the Environment Fund (Fond Environnement) and the National Forest Fund (Fonds National Forêt). They will be enabled to jointly operate a new REDD+ Funding Window and thereby manage and disburse domestic and international resources for the implementation of the Project. The project will also seek resources for broader sectoral needs. The government is committed to progressively increasing its contributions to the REDD+ Fund.

#### 13A. As a result of the significant value of emission reductions generated over the implementation period of Project 1 and, more substantially, over the 20-year lifetime (influence period) of the entire programme, coupled with economic benefits from the transition to diversified agriculture and sustainable forest management practices, La PDR will be able to meet its obligations under the Paris Agreement.

## 8.5.1 GCF Added Value through Complementary Financing of REDD+ Implementation

### 114. Lap PDR has committed sustainable economic resources to participate in REDD+ and to transform its forest sector from a net GHG emission source into a net carbon sink. Lap PDR has been engaged in the REDD+ readiness process since 2013, with the support of the World Bank and other donors. In 2017, the government completed its NDC and its Forestry Strategy (both built upon the National REDD+ Strategy [NRS] and the National REDD+ Vision to 2050 as the official government documents for guiding REDD+ implementation).

### 115. As part of the national NDC process, the government has designed an ERP under the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF) that is aligned with the UNFCCC’s Clean Development Mechanism (CDM) Emission Reductions Payment Agreement (ERP), through which it expects to receive up to USD 42 million of REDD+ results-based financing.

### 116. The FCPF represents the most immediate and concrete source of REDD+ results-based payments. However, other sources may materialize in the future, notably through the UNFCCC Warsaw Framework, AUF/CPCCOP 10 to 12 (the “Warsaw Framework on Enhancing National Determined Contributions”), or what is referred to as the “REDD+ rubicon” of the Warsaw Framework for REDD+. It collates all relevant decisions on successful access and results-based payments, both from the FCPF Carbon Fund but also from other potential sources. The Warsaw Framework for REDD+ includes a number of provisions that indicate that developing countries implementing the five eligible REDD+ activities in phase III have to comply with the rules related to these activities. It is expected that all these rules will be met by 2030, thereby opening up, in principle, future REDD+ financing for Lap PDR not only through the FCPF but also other potential sources.

### 117. In order to receive this results-based financing, investments in the enabling environment, deforestation-free agriculture and sustainable forest landscape management are required. The project is fully aligned with, and explicitly supported by, these requirements.

### Figure 9 shows the forest transition curve for the programme in which Project 1 is embedded. The BAU scenario is given by the Reference Level developed for the ERP. The GCF scenarios capture the impact of avoided GHG emissions from deforestation and forest degradation (DF/FG) under the project, as well as the potential carbon sequestration benefits of sustainable land management practices. This figure provides a graphical illustration of the programme’s added value and paradigm shifting potential, as it shows not only that the project will result in a net reduction of GHG emissions by 2030 (as compared to BAU) but also a sustained post-programme increase thereafter. Such a reversal follows sustained, uninterrupted and precipitous forest decline over the preceding 20 years.

### B.5.2 GCF Added Value through Broad Stakeholder Engagement and Inclusive Design

119. The project applies an innovative approach that aims to empower local actors, including women and members of different ethnic groups, by strengthening their capacities on REDD+ and sustainable land use management, and engaging them not only in planning but also in the implementation, monitoring and enforcement of activities.

### B.5.3 GCF Added Value through Environmental Sustainability

120. Social inclusion is a key element for the success of GCF-supported Outputs, Activities and Actions. Project 1 aims to promote an inclusive approach, ensuring beneficiaries from diverse ethnic groups and marginalized villages are included and empowered. Specific measures include that target their participation and promote their empowerment.

### B.5.4 GCF Added Value through Environmental Sustainability

121. In addition to supporting GHG emission reductions through REDD+, Project 1 will generate substantial additional environmental benefits, including the conservation of natural habitats, increased biological biodiversity and conservation in protected areas, reduced watershed soil erosion and sedimentation, and strengthened ecosystem resilience to climate change impacts and improved land management.

### B.5.5 Financial Analysis

122. The financial analysis focuses on the two Outputs that are amenable to the generation of financial returns, namely Outputs 2 and 3. And the Activities of Output 1 are entirely of a public good nature and will not generate financial returns.

123. Output 2 promotes the transition to sustainable agricultural practices that do not add additional strain on existing forest resources. Most of the GCF funding for Output 2 is concentrated in Activity 2.1, "Local incentives for good agricultural practices". The objective of this activity is to shift 60,000 hectares of land away from two unsustainable agricultural practices: (i) shifting cultivation of upland rice ("non baseline") and (ii) unsustainable maize cultivation ("mazie baseline"). # B.5.6 Economic Analysis

## 127. The following environmental and socio-economic benefits can be expected from GCF-supported interventions: (i) improved ecosystem services; (ii) economic value-added captured by project beneficiaries; and (iii) the health of restored forest ecosystem. The first two factors are quantified in the economic model (Annex 3a). While there is considerable uncertainty about the third factor, it is considered to be a key driver of the overall benefits.

## 128. Sensitivities were run assuming: (i) lower carbon prices (down to the ultra-conservative scenario of Euro 5/tonne in year one and Euro 30/tonne from year two onwards); (ii) higher carbon prices (up to Euro 60/tonne in year one, with a further increase in subsequent years); even at carbon prices of Euro 250/CO2eq (approximately the level reached in the EU-ETS in recent years), the intervention is still economically viable; and (iii) lower discount rates. The model was run by disaggregating emission reductions altogether (i.e., assuming a carbon price of €16) and reducing the value-added captured by project beneficiaries, with the latter scenario being more representative of the program yielding an ERR of 13%.

# B.6. Exit strategy and sustainability

## B.6.1 Financial Exit Strategy

### 130. GCF grant-financing will:

- Enable access to REDD+ results-based payments: Project 1 will close the initial structural funding gap for transforming the forest sector through the provision of GCF grant and deliver emission reductions that are monetized in the form of REDD+ results-based payments (RBP) from the Forest Carbon Fund, through the FCPF Carbon Fund later and further through other potential (e.g. UNFCCC climate), [107]
- Mobilize national and international sustainable forest sector finance: With GCF support, the structure funding gap will be closed and Project 1 will generate significant additional financial streams for the forest and agricultural sectors. Activity 1 of Project 1 is specifically devoted to the mobilization of an army of forest-based enterprises (FBEs) that will be able to access these new sources of finance.
- The total leverage impact of Project 1 is estimated to be Euro 227 million: this includes carbon results-based payments, biodiversity co-benefits, agriculture production, protected areas, forest fires and landslides, and others (see Annex 3 for details).
- Attract private sector investment: The project is designed to facilitate greater private sector investment in the forestry sector. This will be achieved through the creation of a new Forest Carbon Fund (FCF) for sustainable development (Activities 2.1 and 2.2) and mobilizes new and additional investment.

### 130. Strengthening of national financial institutions to operate a REDD+ Funding Window: Project 1 will support the strengthening of the Lao PDR Forest Investment Fund (LPIF), the National Environmental Protection Fund (EPF) and the Forest and Forest Resource Development Fund (FFRDF). They will be enabled to jointly operate a REDD+ Funding Window (RFW) that will channel GCF grant funding, national budget funds, and international sources of finance into FBEs based on markets, both for the GCF project specifically and for other projects in the forest sector. The RFW will also enable the mobilization of additional leverage through the creation of a new Forest Carbon Fund (FCF) that will support carbon-based activities through the LPIF. The FFRDF will be used to provide technical assistance and support to disbursing funds to villages and stakeholders for implementing SFM, FLR and conservation/conservation-free agriculture (Activities 2.1 and 2.2).

The government is expected to progressively increase its contributions to the REDD+ Funding Window, thereby over time replacing the GCF financing. The government's contributions will come from different sources:

- Project 1 will close the initial structural funding gap for transforming the forest sector through the provision of GCF grant and deliver emission reductions that are monetized in the form of REDD+ results-based payments (RBP) from the Forest Carbon Fund, through the FCPF Carbon Fund later and further through other potential (e.g. UNFCCC climate), [107]
- Project 1 will expand these revenue streams by increasing the capacity of FFRDF to channel funds into FBEs.
- Project 1 will use GCF grant funding to unlock FCPF Carbon Fund payments of approximately Euro 23 million in 2025 (with a further Euro 4.8 million in 2026 and final payment in 2029), with FCPF payments under Project 1 amounting to approximately Euro 22 million.
- The Bank will provide technical assistance for the development of the Forest Carbon Fund, which is currently being negotiated by the Government of Lao PDR and the FCPF, but it is expected that the vast majority of these funds will be disbursed through the REDD+ Funding Window. The ER-P (Annex 2b) provides an indicative breakdown of how these funds would be allocated to communities in order to implement similar activities.
- The ER-P (Annex 2a) provides an indicative breakdown of how these funds would be allocated to communities in order to implement similar activities.

The RFW (Annex 3) will channel the GCF grant funding into FBEs, 86% of which would be channeled through the REDD+ Funding Window. The ER-P also outlines some general principles of benefit sharing that the government plans to adopt. It notes that:

- Double counting of emission reductions - understood to involve an emission reduction being counted more than once towards a reduction target - is not allowed under the FCPF rules. However, in order for Lao PDR to receive FCRF revenues for emission reductions, these emission reductions will be retired so that they cannot be 're-used'.

**8.6.2 Exit through Complementing the Government’s Engagement with REDD+ and Alignment with National Policies and Development Plans**

The project is closely aligned with, and explicitly supportive of, government policies and plans (see Section B.1.2). By supporting the implementation of existing national strategies and commitments including Law No. 30/2016, the 8th NATIONAL REDD+ Vision 2020 and the ER-PD, the chances of continuity of impacts and activities after the GCF funding period are high.

**8.6.3 Exit through Alternative Livelihood Opportunities and Private Sector Mobilisation**

Additional income and livelihood opportunities, in conjunction with participatory land use planning and reasonable law enforcement, will create a cascading effect and maintain the low-emission development trajectory triggered by the project. The creation of alternative livelihoods for communities living within or adjacent to proposed interventions is viable in the long-term, with positive net present values as well as many other social, economic, and environmental benefits. In addition, the promotion of agroforestry systems, sustainable agriculture, and deforestation-free value chains will help leverage private sector resources for REDD+ and, ultimately, low-GHG development.

### (d) Other financing arrangements and contributions (max. 0.5 p.a.)

The Government of Lao PDR will contribute co-finance of Euro 11.8 million towards the implementation of Project 1. Key government inputs will be staff time contributions by national, provincial, district, and village level officials. The average annual salary for these positions is on an average government salary at Euro 350/month. The government co-financing contribution also includes in-kind contributions from the Government of Lao PDR.

The beneficiaries of Project 1 (planned to village-level farmer and household time inputs as described above) will contribute co-finance of Euro 62 million towards the implementation of Project 1, including implementation of activities and participation in training in Outputs 2 and 3. Village-level input contributions are expected to be on average at a level equivalent to Euro 50/month per household (Euro 980/000 in total). SME inputs equate to Euro 5.7 million. In total, therefore, beneficiary co-financing contributions will amount to Euro 62 + 5.7 = Euro 67.7 million.

## BMZ financing
The total BMZ financing for the project amounts to Euro 10.8 million. Of this, Euro 5.2 million will be managed through GIZ as an Executing Entity in Laos. GIZ (Executing Entity) will finance part of the project management team (Activity 4.1) and the implementation of Activities 2.3, 2.4, 2.5, 3.1 and 3.2. The remaining Euro 4.8 million of BMZ financing will be implemented by the Government of Laos through the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry (Executing Entity). This includes the implementation of Project Outputs 1 to 7 (Targets 1-6) and Project Target 9 (provinces: Savannakhet and Luang Prabang).

### A2.3, A2.4, A2.5, A3.1, A3.2, A3.3
In the context of a FIDIC EPC it is foreseen to provide technical assistance to support enforcement and land use planning (Activities 1.4 and 1.5). It will also be used for the implementation of Activities 2.1 and 3 in the Project 1 province of Luang Prabang.

### ADB and the EU
ADB will provide co-financing of Euro 12.3 million to implement Activity 2.3. This financing is provided through a loan from the Asian Development Bank (ADB) and a grant from the European Union (EU). The project will be implemented in three provinces: Savannakhet, Champasak and Attapeu. The co-financing will be disbursed based on the implementation overlap of Project 1 and SRWVM in seven districts in three of these provinces (Savannakhet, Champasak and Luang Prabang), where the project and SRWVM will work together.

### The PFDAC FICAS loan project with the Government of Lao PDR
The PFDAC FICAS loan project with the Government of Lao PDR will co-finance Project 1 with Euro 2.5 million. This financing is provided through a loan from the Asian Development Bank (ADB) and a grant from the European Union (EU). The project will be implemented in the Project area. IFAD will provide co-financing direct to the Ministry of Agriculture. The AFD loan is a debt-for-climate-action swap, which means that the Lao government will use the proceeds of this loan to pay off existing debts and will then use the funds for climate action.

### Co-Financiers
Co-financiers will sign contracts with the Lao Government (MUP/MAF) to channels funds to MAF and then onwards to the Departments in the Ministry and its sub-national agencies. The co-financing will be disbursed based on the implementation overlap of Project 1 and SRWVM in seven districts in three provinces: Savannakhet, Champasak and Attapeu.

### Monitoring of VFM
Contributions will be monitored through reporting M&E systems for tracking project Actions, Activities, Outputs and impacts in line with the logical framework, and will ensure that data - including financial information - is collected and reported on a regular basis. Types of evidence for contributions will include, for example, produced standard operating procedures, training materials, field visits, monitoring reports, progress updates, consultations, implemented activities of Annual Village Forest Operational Plans, and others.

### C.3.1 Does the project include capacity building and/or technology transfer? Yes ☑ No ☐

### C.3.2: Does GCF financing fund technology development/transfer? Yes ☒ No ☐

The long-term sustainability of project interventions is enhanced by the project's focus on individual and institutional capacity building, both of the project's implementing entities and the key beneficiaries. Measures focused on sustainable land management practices are expected to be continued in the future within the project regions, given local capacities and the general low level of awareness of sustainable practices. Government entities and the rural communities will continue to benefit from the project's focus on capacity building for improved land use management and REDD+, and it is likely that they will continue to support such measures after project completion. The project will also continue to provide technical assistance, training, and other capacity-building activities during the project life-cycle, as well as the ongoing implementation of such activities after project closure.

### EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

#### D.1 Impact assessment

##### D.1.1 Mitigation

- Detailed GHG calculations are provided in Annex 23.
- The calculations are informed by the detailed analyses undertaken as part of Lao PDR's engagement with REDD+ (the UNFCCC Forest Reference Emission Level [FREL] methodology) and the FCPF Carbon Fund.

##### D.1.2 Forest impacts

- As a result of the direct and enabling implementation activities, over its 4-year implementation period Project 1 is estimated to achieve (relative to the baseline):
    - A reduction in forest degradation of 95%, equivalent to 9,000 ha.
    - A reduction of forest degradation of 10%, equivalent to 9,000 ha.
- An increase of reforestation and restoration of 8% and 1%, respectively—equivalent to 3,160 ha of forest restoration and 245 ha of reforestation.

#### D.2 Paradigm shift potential

##### D.2.1 Potential for scaling-up and replication

- There is substantial potential for scaling up and replicating the programme and its activities (of Projects 1, 2 and 3) in other provinces and municipalities that can be implemented in 6 provinces in Lao PDR (out of 17 provinces and 1 municipality), covering 28 out of 50 districts.
- Within the country, the programme could be replicated to other provinces and municipalities with similar conditions. This would represent a scaling factor of 140%.
- The integration of REDD+ into national and provincial development planning will contribute significantly to the achievement of the Lao PDR’s National Strategy for Climate Change (2nd phase, 2016–2030).

##### D.2.2 Project 1 will enable paradigm shift across different sectors and business activities

- Agriculture, forestry and financial sectors in particular will be targeted as key sectors for transformation.
- Within these sectors, the project will aim to create a new market for carbon credits and other ecosystem services.
- The project will target sectors and commodities where there is strong and consistent demand in order to ensure the success of the programme. Examples of such sectors and commodities have been identified as commodities where demand has existed historically and is expected to increase. More information on these sectors and commodities can be found in the Lao PDR’s National Strategy for Climate Change (2nd phase, 2016–2030).

##### D.2.3 At the national level

- Capacities will be built at the national provincial level for the EPP and FFRDCs, which will facilitate the replication and scale-up of the project as well as the broader distribution of REDD+ finance beyond

### D.2 Potential for knowledge and learning

141. Internationally, the project will provide key lessons learned for supporting private sector development, the adoption of good agricultural practices and sustainable forest management. A similar upland landscape exists in neighboring countries (e.g., Myanmar). The lessons learned from this project's activities can be transferred to these countries as well as other similar regions within Southeast Asia.

### D.2.3 Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

142. Comprehensive communication and exchange of information about topics of crucial relevance for land users, policy makers and other stakeholders will be provided by the project, so that they understand the purpose and benefits of REDD+ and the need for behavioural change of business-as-usual land use. Coordination with the Designated National Authority (DNA) and the BUR will ensure that the project provides a timely and comprehensive provision of relevant information to the DNA or its BUR and National Communication. Communication and collaboration with other projects in the region, such as those under the Lao PDR Component, will be strengthened through the provision of materials, as well as to support these universities in acquiring research projects related to REDD+. Ensuring the development of a strong enabling environment for REDD+ implementation is also supported by the project via a train-of-trainers approach and delivered by the extension staff of DAFO, at the district level. The knowledge management system will be strengthened through the establishment of a database.

### D.2.3 Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies

143. Project 1 contains a strong enabling environment element, including policy and regulatory reform (notably, Activities 1.2 and 1.3), support to regulatory enforcement (Activity 1.4) and improved land use planning (Activity 1.5). This will contribute to the development of a new forest management law (FMA) and a new GMG agricultural extension system (e.g., Activity 2.1, among others). Project 1 also seeks to support the financing environment for REDD+ in Lao PDR by developing a new forestry sector policy (Activity 1.6), as well as by providing technical assistance to a new green line credit for deforestation-free agriculture that is being set up and supported by the World Bank. The project will also seek to ensure that the new law is enabled through carbon MRV system.

145. Component 3 will seek to support the scaling up of REDD+ activities in other countries with similar or neighboring countries with similar landscape forestry contexts. D

## Environmental benefits for REDD+ implementation over the 20-year programme lifetime.

It can be said that the 1.8 million ha of forest landscapes, which otherwise would be subject to deforestation and degradation pressures, will avoid deforestation and degradation in the next 20 years. This is equivalent to a reduction of CO2 emissions for 20 years, with the effect that 147.4 million tCO2eq in emissions are prevented over the 20-year programme lifetime.

The above estimate assumes that the "global beneficiary" would have to procure this quantity of emission reductions through carbon credits under the Clean Development Mechanism (CDM) Scheme, where credits trade at US$ 6 per tonne CO2. See Annex 3b for the assumptions and methodology used.

### The local beneficiary in Laos: nearly 250,000 direct beneficiaries and a further 420,000 affected indirectly

All benefits will be shared by the Lao PDR government and its people. The programme, through its promotion of shifts in cultivation to more forest-friendly approaches, provides a just transition for smallholder farmers who are currently dependent on slash-and-burn agriculture, providing them with land and work migration to neighbouring economies. The economic net present value (ENPV) provided to the local beneficiary is estimated at US$ 10 billion.

### SMEs create multiple effects

The companies will not only invest in better crops, education and equipment. They will also need the services of accountants, suppliers of office space, and related services, which create additional employment opportunities for the local population. See Annex 2a for further details on Project 1. For instance, 160 SMEs will receive training in good agricultural practices and business management, leading to an increase in productivity by 35% over a period of five years. This will lead to increased revenues to approximately 18,000 producers and provide employment to approximately 16,000 people.

### Gender-sensitive development benefits

In addition to avoiding the continuation of existing poor stereotypes and practices that are harmful to women's rights and empowerment, the programme in which Project 1 is embedded offers significant potential to contribute positively to gender equality. Project 1 will work with local communities to ensure that women have access to land and other resources. It also raises awareness among learners targeting women as carriers of traditional knowledge and change agents within their families and communities, empowering them to participate more fully in decision-making at all levels. Whenever possible, project-supported awareness-raising village consultations, training, and discussions will be held with women's groups. The programme will provide improved participation of women and empower them to take on more leadership roles in local structures, including the Community Forest Management Committees (CFMCs) and the Women's Groups, which will include a quota of at least 30% female committee members. Project 1's provision of Village Development Funds for each CFMC will ensure that women have access to credit and other resources necessary to start small enterprises or support joint decision-making and benefiting women and poor households.

To support women to fully participate in the project, it is proposed that a Women's Group be established at the village level. This will help to address common participatory gaps and barriers. The project will further expand new economic opportunities for women by providing training on gender-sensitive business planning, incorporating gender assessments in value chain studies, and providing targeted technical and financial support (within the context of the project) to women's groups and individual women entrepreneurs. Project 1 also aims at gender equality and social inclusion, including time-saving activities.

### Ethnic-sensitive development benefits

Specific needs of ethnic and indigenous groups include access to land, forest and natural resources for livelihood purposes; respect for their cultural rights and practices; and access to education and health services in their own language. The project will work with these communities by providing training on gender equality and social inclusion (including small group meetings with appropriate translators or ethnic language speakers) on proposed activities – leading to the participation of women and men from all ethnic groups. It is also proposed that land use plans with land access are incorporated in Participatory Land Use Plans (PLUPs), sensitive to customary uses of land.

#### Project contributions to the Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs):

- **SDG-13: Climate Action** – By reducing significant amounts of GHG emissions, implementing NDC actions at scale, and supporting countries in integrating climate change into national policies, building climate-related capacity, and raising awareness on climate change mitigation.
  - **SDG-1: No Poverty** – Through generating improved livelihoods and income for rural poor population groups.
  - **SDG-2: Zero Hunger** – By improving agricultural productivity and incomes of small-scale food producers, in particular women farmers, youth, indigenous peoples, and people with disabilities; increasing access to productive resources and inputs, knowledge, financial services, markets and opportunities for value-addition and non-farm employment.
  - **SDG-3: Good Health** – Through promoting women’s participation and decision-making concerning opportunities in land-use and natural resources, as well as economic and entrepreneurial activities.
  - **SDG-8: Decent Work and Economic Growth** – By promoting private sector-oriented policies that support productive enterprises, including small-scale enterprises; improving access to finance for micro-enterprises by supporting the formalisation and growth of micro-, small- and medium-sized enterprises, including through access to financial services.
  - **SDG-12: Sustainable Production and Consumption** – By supporting the sustainable management and efficient use of natural resources (notably forests and land).
  - **SDG-15: Life on Land** – By contributing to the implementation of sustainable management of all types of terrestrial areas, deforestation, restoring degraded forests and substantially increasing afforestation and reforestation.
  - **SDG-17: Partnerships for the Goals** – By strengthening domestic resource mobilisation, including through enhanced tax administration capacity; improving public financial management capacity for tax and other revenue collection; and mobilising additional financial resources from multiple sources.

#### Project contributions to the 3 pillars of sustainable development:

- **Economic pillar**: Improved livelihoods and income for rural poor population groups; support to SMEs; and strengthened domestic resource mobilisation.
- **Social pillar**: Improved access to education, nutrition and health through improved agricultural productivity; improved employment opportunities, including for women and ethnic groups; and improved gender equality through economic training and entrepreneurial activities.
- **Environmental pillar**: Reduction of GHG emissions in the forestry sector and increased carbon sequestration from the atmosphere; and sustainable management and efficient use of natural resources (notably forests and land).

#### D.4 Needs of recipient

##### D.4.1 Climate vulnerability

The project is a mitigation (REDD+) initiative. Nonetheless, it will serve to address climate vulnerabilities in Lao PDR. In its Second National Communication to the UNFCCC, Laot PDR identifies itself as an LDC with limited adaptive capacity and high exposure to climate change impacts. The Enhanced Action Programme for Southeast Asia (EAPSEA) ranks Lao PDR as one of the most climate-vulnerable countries in the


---
**Page 63**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 59 OF 95

## 155\. The annual mean temperature in Southeast Asia consistently increased between 1970 to 2005. From 1991 to 2005, the number of days with temperatures exceeding 38°C has increased by more than 40%. In contrast, the number of days below freezing point has decreased by about 60% during this period. The annual mean precipitation in Southeast Asia also revealed increased seasonal (2,948 mm/year) and annual (2,741 mm/year) rainfall rates. These trends are due to increasing frequency of extreme weather events such as tropical cyclones, floods, droughts, and wildfires.

## 156\. From 1996-2008, about three-quarters of national disasters were climate-related (flood, storm, 43%, drought 14%). The frequency of natural disasters has increased from once every two years before 1992 to once per year over the past decade. These trends are due to increasing frequency and intensity of extreme weather events such as tropical cyclones and wildfires, a median risk for extreme heat, and a low risk for water scarcity.

## 157\. By 2010, mean annual temperatures in projected to increase by +3°C (+4°C under high emissions scenarios). The proportion of extreme rainfall is projected to increase by about 9 days from 1990 to 2100. The duration of heat waves to increase from less than 10 to more than 150 days on average in 2100. Also, under high emissions projections the proportion of drought days is projected to increase from 50 days in 1990 to about 65 in 2100.

## 158\. In northern Latin America specifically, high-resolution (0.05 km) climate models using interpolation of observed values, and the regional scale IPCC Special Report on Emissions Scenarios (SRES) scenarios as input into General Circulation Models (GCMs), applied to major catchments in the north of the country predict a temperature increase of 0.8-0.9°C relative to current conditions by 2030. Precipitation may increase slightly (11–15 mm annual average) but with greater temporal variability.

## 159\. The project design responds to these climate changes in a number of ways:

* **Inclusion of drought-resistant crops and varieties, and support to dry-season irrigation schemes**, in Output 2.

## Capacity building and training on sustainable water harvesting techniques, risk mitigation processes (including good agricultural practices with strong climate change adaptation co-benefits that can reduce risk): for example, the use of contour bunds, terraces, and other soil conservation measures to reduce erosion and sedimentation that contribute to riverbank cutting and inverted river downwelling as well as sustainable pest management.

- Capacity building for farmers on sustainable pest and disease management
- Land cover mapping and analysis to identify areas with high exposure to risk (e.g. identifying high-risk areas for landslides, flooding, etc.), and will support the planning, adoption and monitoring of sustainable land management practices

The timing of project activities will be conducted considering seasonal conditions, climate, etc. (e.g. tree planting).

Regular monitoring conducted within the framework of the project will lead to early detection, follow-up and the implementation of corrective measures. The project will also include a dedicated team of staff member dedicated to monitoring the impact of the project and implementation of the Environmental and Social Management Plan.

## D.4.2 Financial, economic, social and institutional needs

### 160. Lao PDR is a landlocked least developed country (LLDC) that is ranked 139th out of 189 on the Human Development Index. Northern Lao PDR has historically been the poorest and most rural region of the country.

The average per capita income in the target area is approximately USD 1,200 per capita, compared with a national average of USD 2,336.14

- In total, over 1.8 million people live in the target provinces. Over the past decade, the provincial population has been increasing at an annual rate of 5%, which is higher than the national average (3%) and even higher than other centrales15, which is below the national average.16 In northern Lao PDR, poverty rates are among the highest in the region. The poverty headcount ratio for the target area was estimated at 40% of the population between 2009 and 2013.17 Despite this notable progress, poverty levels in the northern region still exceed the national average (35%).18

- In addition to poverty, other factors such as high vulnerability to climate change, low Human Development Index (HDI) scores and Boko and Haukoune have particularly low HDI values. Food security is a major challenge for many households in rural areas of northern Lao PDR. The majority of the population lives below the national poverty line due to poor19, and households in the agricultural sector are twice as likely than non-farm households to fall back into poverty.

### 162. The project seeks to remove barriers that currently prevent Lao stakeholders from accessing climate and REDD+ finance to help address their financial, economic and social institutional needs:

- Barrier: Government budgets are constrained and highly dependent upon donor support, partly because collection of revenue is low (e.g., the tax base in northern Lao PDR is very small compared with other land uses) and taxes are not collected efficiently.
- Project Objective: The GCF project will build the legislative, enforcement and institutional capabilities of Government entities – notably the EPF and FPRDF – to collect forest fees and taxes, to diversify revenue sources and to improve the efficiency of tax collection in northern Lao PDR.
- Barrier: Lao PDR is a landlocked LLDC that faces a high risk of external debt distress. The fiscal space for additional Government borrowing is extremely limited.

The GCF project will channel considerable co-finance to the forestry sector, including grant support from the Governments of Germany and Japan, and concessional loans from ADB and IFAD.

## Barrier: The beneficiary groups - village foresters and subsistence farmers - are among the poorest populations in the region. They lack access to markets for their products, limited access to markets and financial services, and they have insufficient assets for the repayment of loans.
Response: The GCF project will set up and operationalize a “REDD+ Funding Window” as the key national funding mechanism for the forestry sector. This window will be designed in accordance with international standards. The REDD+ Funding Window will be operated by the EPPF, and progressively, the FFRDF, thereby leveraging their capacities and experience to deliver finance.

D.5.4 Engagement with the NDA, civil society organisations and other relevant stakeholders

Stakeholder engagement has formed a central element of project design: over one thousand stakeholders have taken part in GCF project consultation meetings. Stakeholders have played an important role in providing feedback on the draft project document (including the proposed project development plan) and popular programmes). Endorsement engagement with stakeholders has also been conducted for the elaboration of the ER/ IP, which is currently under way, and will provide a strong foundation for the design of GCF Project 1.

Stakeholder engagement within the framework of the GCF GCF

Preliminary scoping missions
167. Preliminary scoping missions were conducted by GIZ staff and project development experts to assess the possibility of developing a GCF concept note and potential funding proposal from April 3-7 and April 23-30, 2018. Mission participants identified key issues related to the proposed project design, including: the geography; a possible product; whilst ensuring the relevance of the concept selected. A high-level meeting with government representatives was held on May 9th, which provided initial feedback from the main (project partners and supporting parties) interested in providing co-finance.

168. The National REDD+ Vision establishes the strategic objectives for Laos PDR’s struggle in forest protection and expansion: the National REDD+ Strategy is a key document that sets out the strategy to achieve these objectives, including the identification of priority areas (e.g., protected areas, agricultural land, infrastructure and mining), timber harvesting, shifting cultivation and forest fires; and commercial tree plantations. The strategy also identifies the institutional arrangements for implementing the strategy, including the roles and responsibilities of relevant stakeholders (e.g., government agencies, civil society organizations, private sector actors); and the financing mechanisms to support implementation. The National REDD+ Vision addresses 4 of the key forest challenges and is aligned primarily with strategic programmes 1-4 and 5, with a contribution to 3.

### Engagement with the NDA (MNR)

The project has been developed with regular engagement from the NDA (MNR) in Lao PDR. The idea to develop a REDD+ proposal to support the Emission Reductions Programme (ERP) was taken on by the NDA and officially presented at the GCF Structured Dialogue in June 2017. Since then, the project has formed part of the NDA’s work plan for 2018-2023. The project team has held several meetings with multi-stakeholder workshops to provide feedback on the project and the broader programme. A letter of no objection has been received from the NDA in support of the project, which is aligned with the country's national priorities, strategies and plans, and that it is in accordance with relevant laws and regulations.

### Engagement with government focal points for REDD+ and the UNFCCC

In addition to maintaining ongoing communication with the NDA, the project has been designed with the continued support of the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry (MoAF) as a key stakeholder, where the government has demonstrated strong ownership of the project concept. The head of the country's REDD+ Division and the National REDD+ Forum have provided guidance on the project design and implementation through regular high-level meetings and stakeholder consultations. The UNFCCC Focal Point within MoAF has also been kept informed about the project, with representatives from MAMIE having regularly participated in project consultation events and workshops.

### Engagement with development partners

A multi-donor working group was set up to support the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry on REDD+ issues. The working group includes key donors such as the World Bank (including representatives of the SUFORD project and the FCPF Technical Assistance team), JICA and FAO. The working group has assisted MoAF with the development of the FCPF-Lao PDR Project Proposal, which was submitted to the FCPF in 2016. The project is also supported by the World Bank – both the Lao Country Office and the FCFFP - through the REDD+ development process, starting in 2015 with the submission of a Concept Note and culminating in the preparation of the Project Proposal. H.E. Thongvong Vongmya wrote to letter to BM Minister Dr. Gerd Mueller to request GIZ support to design and implement the project, including other development partners as potential co-financers.

### Engagement with Civil Society Organizations

CSOs have been consulted extensively, and at frequent intervals, during the project preparation process. CSOs involved in the design of the project include the Lao Women’s Union (including at national, provincial and district levels), the National Association of Rural Women (NARW), the Lao Farmers’ Federation, the Lao CSO Network and the National University of Laos (NUOL). CSOs represent a central beneficiary of project support as they will be involved in the implementation of the project. The proposed Community Forest Management Plans and Village Forest Management Agreements (VFMAs) will be subject to prior village-level discussion and agreement, and LCFM will provide technical assistance for the preparation of these documents. CSOs will play an important role in various aspects of the project:

- **CSOs will benefit from capacity-building events, training, awareness-raising and project activities.**
- **CSOs such as farmer-based associations will facilitate communication between local communities and the project management units.**

## Stakeholder engagement and project implementation

### Project Implementation Plan (PIP)

#### Section D: Cost-effectiveness and co-financing

##### D.1 Cost-effectiveness of the project

The project is estimated to result in a 3 million tonne reduction in CO2 emissions over the four-year project implementation period.

This equates to a mitigation cost to the GCF of €2 per tonne of CO2 (see Section E.2). For reference, the investment cost of another approved GCF REDD+ Phase 2 project (PF19, Ecuador) is Euro 2.5/MtCO2e.

##### D.3 Co-financing and leveraging

Co-finance:
- Project 1 GCF budget: Euro 15,180,596
- Project 1 co-finance: Euro 48,830,495

# D.3 Financial assumptions

## 178. The Activities under Output 1 of Project 1 are entirely of a public good nature and will not generate financial returns.

These Outputs are naturally suited to a grant. Outputs 2 and 3 promote the adoption of sustainable agricultural practices (e.g., agroforestry, conservation agriculture, intercropping, crop rotation, mixed cropping), agroforestry and sustainable forest management (utilisation of non-timber forest products and timber harvesting in a manner that does not compromise future productivity) and the use of improved seeds and inputs to current baseline (unsustainable) practices. The target beneficiaries are, however, subsistence farmers facing substantial financial constraints. This makes co-financing for these activities almost impossible under the project and GEF grant. Significant co-finance, both with grants and loans, is mobilised in the context of the project.

## 179. In addition, the government's ability to assume additional debt obligations is severely constrained by the Lao fiscal and debt situation. Laos has recorded budget deficits in the range of approximately 3-5% of GDP each year since 2008. The ratio of total public debt to GDP (including external debt) as a percentage of GDP, which was 46% in 2015, has increased from 56% in 2013 to its projected 66% in 2019. The IMF recommends, as a priority, the reduction of the fiscal deficit through a reduction of the budget deficit and other measures.

## D.4 Application of best practices

### 180. Land use practices commonly applied in the project area are characterised by low productivity and cannot typically be considered good agricultural practice. Increased competition for commercial agricultural lands is shifting land uses from subsistence agriculture (e.g., rice, maize, soybeans, cassava) to less suitable forested upland areas. Tree-cutting and increasing land degradation are leading to lower productivity of the remaining agricultural land. The lack of access to markets and limited access to forest degradation. Poor access to irrigation exacerbates the low productivity of agricultural production, as does the absence of appropriate soil conservation measures and other sustainable land management practices (e.g., soil conservation measures, climate-resilient practices) due to limited awareness of villagers about these issues.

### 181. The UNFCCC Technology Needs Assessment (TNA) explicitly identifies agriculture (alongside forestry) as one of two key sectors for climate change mitigation and is also one of the two key sectors (alongside water) for climate change adaptation. Mitigation measures include improved land use practices, such as agroforestry, forested reforestation, irrigation, climate-smart agriculture and crop diversification, that are addressed by Project 1 which also includes a component on climate change adaptation. Adaptation measures include the use of information from modern technology (such as GPS, remote sensing and GIS).

# E.4 Activities for Project 1

## 1.1 REDD+ Funding Window and sustainable finance

### Description
Establishment and operationalisation of a REDD+ Funding Window (FW) in EPF to provide financial support to FFRFs, including the process: provide EPF to FFRFs with funding from EPF's GCF as accreditation for the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF) to channel finance for REDD+ activities.

### Sub-activities
1.1.1 Establishment of REDD+ Funding Window in EPF
   - Creation of a REDD+ Funding window in EPF, including operational guidelines and internal guidance documents.
   - Development of the technical and safeguarding standards and procedures, SOPs, manuals and other relevant documents that meet international best practices and safeguards standards.

### Deliverables
REDD+ Funding Window is established and operationalised with financial resources (both national and international) amounting to at least Euro 8 million. Staff in the EPF's day-to-day operations are trained on the REDD+ Funding Window.

## 1.2 Maintaining REEDD+ into the NDC and socio-economic development plans (SEDPs)

### Description
Provision of technical and logistical support to the NDC secretariat to ensure the National REDD+ Action Plan (NAP) is maintained into the updated NDC in line with national and district SEDPs.

### Sub-activities
1.2.1 Development of a framework for Activities 2.1, 2.3 & 2.4
   - Developed and agreed with stakeholders on structure and mobilise funds from various IPs (from the agriculture industry, road fees, taxes, etc.)
   - Develop a National REDD+ Action Plan (NAP) Development Framework.
   - Additional coordination and logistical support to a green credit line that only provides funding in the framework of the project's GCF-REDD+ funds to firms for zero-emission forest management.

### Support from Activity 2.2
1.2.2 National level consultation workshops
   - Consultation workshops on NAP development.
   - Additional coordination and logistical support to a green credit line that only provides funding in the framework of the project's GCF-REDD+ funds to firms for zero-emission forest management.

## 1.3 Strengthening the regulatory framework

### Description
Revision, modification and drafting of relevant laws, regulations and policies to enable and strengthen the legal framework for participatory village forestry.

### Sub-activities
1.3.1 International and national expert inputs
   - Consultation workshops on NAP development.
   - Additional coordination and logistical support to a green credit line that only provides funding in the framework of the project's GCF-REDD+ funds to firms for zero-emission forest management.

### Deliverables
The revised and endorsed by Government Forest Law, Land Law as well as supporting policies, amended to accommodate for a promotion forests.

### Sustainable rural infrastructure: watershed management

The GCF project complements ADB investment in sustainable irrigation schemes through the development of forested landscapes in the upper catchment areas. The project will address drivers of deforestation (such as agricultural practices) and land degradation, and reduce vulnerability to climate change by promoting alternative livelihoods that improve soil productivity; lack of value chain linkages; and weak negotiation/marketing skills.

#### 3.1 Village forest management

Development, implementation and monitoring of village forest management plans (VFMPS)

- **VMFPs**: including development of detailed village forest resource assessments (PFRA), tree inventories, preparation of annual operation plans and budgetary proposals.
- **Gender-equitable and responsive planning processes** (including representation of women on Village Forest Management Committees).
- **Implementation, monitoring and reporting**: establishment of VMFP by village groups; Village Group action plan for operation (approved by GCF and VFMPC); Quarterly monitoring of implementation of VMFPs; Annual reports to VDFC, reports of illegal activities in the annual report.

Forest inventory and forest management planning in accordance with the implementation of Forest Productivity Improvement (FPI) activities associated with associated monitoring

* Desegregation of villages and preparation and submission of improved forest management plans (EMPs) for FPI activities, zoning, boundary redefinition, development of guidelines to the public-private partnership
* Activity 2.1: Dissemination and enforcement of forest success stories in conjunction with all stakeholders, development of guidelines for villages and provincial level individuals on FPIs, monitoring for EMPS, inventory revision, remote-sensing and aerial surveying and enforcement (patrolling activities), preparation of annual work and budget plans to implement FPI activities, restoration activities, including community-based conservation and community-based sustainable forest management development
* RECONSIDER Window 3 funding forestry grants to VCFs. Prioritize funding gender-equitable management plans and gender sharing of VCFs

15,000 ha of production forest category area under improved forest management

* Implementation of law enforcement action plans including community-based operation, targeting of high-risk areas by D&D staff, capacity building and training of employment, gender

### 4.1 Project management, coordination, monitoring and reporting by the GIZMU and PMU

#### Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements

182. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation of project outputs in line with the ESMP and QAP will comply with the relevant GCF policies and procedures.
183. The project will apply a customised results-based Monitoring and Evaluation (M&E) system. The system will be based on:
    - GIZ Standard Operating Procedures ("GIZ's evaluation policy, principles, guidelines and requirements")
    - The Project’s logical framework
    - The Project 1 year implementation schedule
    - Requirements of the GCF’s Annual Performance Report
    - Development of a project-specific M&E procedures.
    - Procedures and requirements of project partners and stakeholders in Laos

The M&E system will be used to monitor progress, outputs and impacts as well as associated financial flows across all Outputs in all project districts and provinces.

185. The overall responsibility and oversight for M&E and reporting lies with the GCF AE unit at GIZ head office. The National Project Management Unit (NPMU) in Laos will implement the M&E system and work closely with Provincial

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL (2/3) PAGE 80 OF 95

## Recruitment of M&E staff

186. Immediately when Project 1 commences, GIZ’s GEZ AE unit at head office in Germany will make available one expert to support the Project and manage the project M&E and reporting routines. He/she will cooperate closely with NPM(s) and GIZ Executing Entity staff to coordinate the implementation of the project's M&E system. As soon as GCF project procedures are finalized, GIZ’s GEZ AE unit at head office in Germany will make available one expert to support the Project 1 M&E system. The experts will be based in Berlin.

187. In addition, GIZ’s GEZ AE unit at head office in Germany will have an international consultant to support the management of the M&E system and provide on-the-job training for NPM(s) and GIZ Executing Entity staff. The consultant will also assist with the development of the M&E system.

188. In addition, GIZ’s GEZ AE unit at head office in Germany will have an international consultant to support the implementation of the M&E system in their respective province and districts. In the first year of Project 1, this expert will be based in Berlin. In subsequent years, he/she will be based in the provinces and districts designing the details of the M&E system and support each early implementation phase. The firm will also provide training for NPM(s) and GIZ Executing Entity staff.

## Independent monitoring and evaluation studies

189. GIZ’s GEZ AE unit may make available an MTR (in year three of Project 1 at any time that GIZ, the NDA and/or the National Steering Committee consider necessary). GIZ will competitively select and assign an independent consultant to conduct a mid-term review (MTR) with the objective of identifying target groups and beneficiaries, project partners and developing contribution partners. The MTR will include:

- A review of the institutional, administrative, organisational, environmental, social, economic, technical and financial aspects of the Project 1 implementation in the provinces and districts (as specified in the Funding Proposal and Feasibility Study) and M&E system.
- A review of the project’s progress against the approved programme and whether it can be changed or waived due to altered conditions.
- A review of the viability of remaining planned impacts.
- An assessment of the need to restructure or reformulate the programme (including subsequent consultants’ opinions on the extent to which the programme’s objective and medium-term goals are being achieved).
- The timing of the MTR is designed such that lessons learned from implementation of Project 1 mid-2020 to mid-2024 can be used in the preparation of the next phase.

190. GIZ’s GEZ AE unit will make available an MTR report to the GCF Secretariat and project stakeholders.

## Data collection and frequency

190. The NPM(s) will coordinate data collection for implemented activities through responsible executive departments / divisions at the district and provincial level. The NPM(s) will supervise and guide the monitoring and evaluation. Each NPM(s) will be responsible for ensuring that all relevant data is collected, recorded and reported to GIZ’s GEZ AE unit at head office and make a summary report available to GIZ’s GEZ AE unit at head office, again at least every six months.

## Measurement, Reporting and Verification (MRV) of reduced GHG emissions

191. The MRV will be used as the basis for measuring the Project 1 implementation’s efficacy and effectiveness as well as for continued learning across the programme. In cooperation with JICA, Project I includes a dedicated technical assistance component to support the development of an MRV system. The MRV system will be specifically dedicated to the measurement and reporting of achieved emission reductions. The MRV will be subject to independent verification by third-party experts (e.g., international consultants). Methodologically, the same approach will be used as for the Reference Level development (see Section D 1.1).
