# Risk Assessment and Management

## F.1 Risk identification and mitigation measures

### Selected Risk Factor 1 (Technical and operational)

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |

**Description**

Sub-optimal cross-sectoral coordination and potential conflicting interests may limit the adoption and effectiveness of REDD+ related measures.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)

* The GCF project builds on strengthened relationships and cross-sector planning established through the REDD+ Task Force, and promotes continuous dialogue and ongoing capacity building and coordination. It will continue to strengthen intersectoral collaboration by engaging key actors in the development of the ER-PD and PRAPs, during recent years.
* Improved sectoral planning (including demarcation of land, issuance of GIS maps, participatory land use planning and forest management plans) will ensure that REDD+ activities are integrated into existing land-use plans based on uses.
* Improved monitoring and enforcement of forest areas, including areas with approved timber harvesting permits, will increase accountability and enforcement, ultimately limiting over-harvesting and unsustainable forest use.

### Selected Risk Factor 2 (Technical and operational)

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Low |

**Description**

High staff turnover and limited numbers of government extension staff impedes retention of skills and knowledge in the relevant sectors/institutions.

#### Mitigation Measure(s)

* The project places a strong emphasis on training, awareness dissemination – as detailed in the Activity Descriptions in Chapter 3 of the Feasibility Study (Annex 2a) and in the project’s knowledge management framework – and continuous engagement with relevant authorities at the national, province and district levels will help to strengthen knowledge exchange and maintain organizational knowledge. National and provincial PBOs will support this exchange.
* Training of multiple staff members in each department/province/district, etc., will mitigate the risk of any one of them leaving.

### Selected Risk Factor 3 (Technical and operational)

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Low | Medium |


---
**Page 86**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 83 OF 95

## Description

Private sector actors do not want to participate in the project and prefer to continue using BAU practices.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

- Extensive consultations with leading private sector actors have been undertaken during the GCF project development stage to gauge their interest in participating. Leading forestry companies, such as Burapha Agroforestry Ltd., have expressed their interest in scaling-up commercial forest management activities and are interested in exploring new markets for products such as cardamom and galangal, which are compatible with agroforestry systems and sustainable agriculture.
- Agricultural value chain actors such as rice millers and traders have indicated their interest in training and capacity building to improve their business practices (e.g., good agricultural practices, quality assurance, accounting/bookkeeping), as well as matchmaking with other value chain actors (dairies, local and international buyers). These actors are also interested in improving their access to finance.
- Therefore, it is of key interest to private sector stakeholders.

### Selected Risk Factor 4

Category: Governance  
Probability: Medium  
Impact: Low  

Description:

The project will provide dedicated support to the FFRDF (Activity 1.1), including support to redesign of the FFRDF’s governance structure, its standard operating procedures, and its manuals and guidance documents so that they meet international standards (e.g., the IFC Performance Standards). This will be done in close collaboration with capacity development (including on-the-job exposure to the GCF-Funding Window) to build needed skills and address existing gaps.

### Selected Risk Factor 5

Category: Governance  
Probability: Medium  
Impact: Medium  

Description:

Utilisation of the Environmental Protection Fund (EPF) as a back-up strategy: the project has been designed to utilise the EPF as an alternative funding mechanism in case the REDD+ Fund is not available. The systems are already in place and meet international standards (see the EPF capacity assessment, Annex 22). It is intended that the EPF will be used only if it becomes necessary for the project to do so because of a lack of its capacities and systems are in place. However, if this is not possible the EPF will continue to manage the interim REDD+ Fund as a back-up and the project will continue to channel resources through the EPF.

### Selected Risk Factor 6

Category: Governance  
Probability: Medium  
Impact: Low  

Description:

Participating households do not comply with land use plans that are developed. Ethnic groups and poor households could be especially affected, as they are the most likely to experience economic dislocation due to their reliance on the land.


---
**Page 87**
---

# Mitigation Measure(s)

* Participation in the project’s activities will be voluntary and based on the principle of FPIC.
* The project's grievance mechanism will be clearly communicated in culturally appropriate ways, and villagers will be able to access it.

## Land use plans

* Land use plans will be developed using participatory stakeholder processes and will be based on the priorities and goals identified by stakeholders. These plans will ensure that land use is equitable for all households, including those of marginalised and vulnerable households.
* Awareness will be raised among households on current agricultural practices and their impacts on soil fertility and water quality.

## Incentives

* Incentives will be provided to help overcome opportunity costs and support the transition to sustainable land use (e.g. through provision of inputs, training, or access to credit). These incentives may also include additional financial sources for future investments in sustainable land use).

### Capacity Building

* Capacity building activities will focus on knowledge dissemination, training/capacity building and awareness-raising will help improve compliance and adoption.
* External communication tools such as posters, brochures, videos, pictures and other tools to communicate project content, including translations into key languages (as necessary). Stakeholder consultations on proposed regulatory measures will be held in advance of their implementation. These consultations will seek to sustain community interest and commitment to the project through active participation and compliance, and religious leaders will be involved where appropriate.

### Strengthened capacities

* Strengthened capacities and technical support for ongoing monitoring will assess potential trade-offs or unforeseen consequences that may identify the need for potential adjustments.

## Selected Risk Factor 6

### Technical/Operational

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Description | Parts of the project area are contaminated with Unspecified Ordinance (UXO), which poses a potential risk for agriculture and linear infrastructure. |

### Mitigation Measure(s)

* Within the project area, Unspecified UXOs were found in Huanan and Luang Prabang provinces. The same applies for the province of Sayaboury, though they are not considered to be a significant risk as there have been no reported incidents involving UXO (Output 2 and 3), but it is classified as low. Across Laos there are fewer than 50 casualties per year, down from 1976 to present day. The project area has experienced no UXO-related casualties in the past decade. No UXOs were recorded with critical incidents in the context of their project in recent years, including agriculture, forestry and infrastructure projects. However, there is a risk that UXO may be found during construction activities or other land use activities. There are no known UXO risks. However, certain agriculture- and forest-related activities require disturbing the ground (for example, harvesting bamboo) which could potentially disturb UXOs. The project area has been surveyed for UXO contamination.
* The following measures have proven to work for development agencies in Laos and the GCF project will ensure they are conducted as part of this project:
    * Confirmed clearance of UXOs with provincial authorities before commencing project activities.
    * Impact assessments based on historical bombing data as well as on the Information Management System for Mines (IMSM) database. The IMSM is available online at <https://www.mine.gov.la/imsm>.
* Applying NRA UXO guidelines and other standardised resources available online at <http://www.nra.gov.la/resources.html>
    * If needed, clearance of UXOs can be initiated through the Government's National Programme for UXO Clearance. The project will ensure that any such action is undertaken in consultation with the relevant authorities.
* Community-based Mine Risk Education activities to offer people knowledge and alternatives for living and working safely in mine-UXO contaminated areas (available online at <http://www.nra.gov.la/resources.html>)

GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (ESIA)

#### G.1.1 Environmental and Social Impact Assessment (ESIA)
193. The ESIA rates the risk category of the project as B (medium).

194. The activities under Project 1 are oriented around FPCs, positively helping small-scale farmers and with a focus on ethnic groups in remote locations. A detailed description of the project’s FPC process is provided in the Ethical Guidelines for Community Engagement in the Context of Forest Projects (2018). This process will be guided by a Community Engagement Framework (CEF), which the GCF project will adopt. The ESIA notes that the Project (which is proposed to be implemented under the Forest Tax Funds (FTFs) or Provincial REDD+ Offices [PROs]), which will facilitate stakeholder consultations.

196. Particular challenges identified by the ESIA include:
- Some studies conducted by MAP and MDFRE via its REEDS module indicates that sub-national technical skills of government agencies to implement improvement Districts, particularly in remote areas, are often underdeveloped.
- Under-budgeted and under-equipped. District Agriculture and Forest Offices (DAFOs) do not necessarily have anyone who has had more than minimal exposure to the project and climate change concepts.

197. Among the 2018 World Bank Doing Business Report ranks Laos 154 out of 190 in previous years it was ranked 141 and 139. The business environment is still weak, with a score of 62 (out of 100). This score is significantly lower than most scores in Laos compared to other countries. The regulatory framework on doing business in the agricultural and forestry sectors is not yet fully developed. However, the government has made significant progress in this area.

198. The government has recognized that corruption is a matter of serious concern in the country. The Party Congress Resolution No 02-NQ/TW (2016) on Anti-Corruption and Prevention of Corruption has been issued. However, but it will be a long process to ensure that all regulatory and inspection frameworks are in place and also to ensure that they are effectively implemented. The World Bank Doing Business Report ranks Laos 154 out of 180, with a score of 28 (out of 100). The projects financial monitoring, reporting and auditing systems will be required to mitigate potential issues.

199. The traditional practice of land allocation and resource allocation at district level means that land titles are still confined largely to urban and peri-urban areas in Laos PDR. In rural areas, there are over two million land parcels with no title or certificate. This is a major challenge for the project as it will be difficult to ensure that all claims on land are settled in practice. While there is some recognition in law for communal (cooperative) tenure, the issues of land allocation and resource provision has yet to take place. Project support and capacity development in this regard will be vital.

200. The project area represents a diverse set of socio-economic, cultural and environmental conditions. Thus, risks associated with the project are likely to vary significantly across different areas within the project site as such a complex project area. The complex and interacting dynamics of landscapes, ethnic groups and policy implementation will make it difficult for the project to be implemented in an equitable manner that does not complicate people’s livelihoods. The socio-economic risks that may arise during the implementation of the project are likely to be more significant than those associated with other projects. This is due to the importance of the site-specific Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) that has been developed for this project, which will ensure that all stakeholders have a say in how the project is implemented. The ESMP that will be set up during project implementation. The high percentage of non-Lao Tzu ethnic groups in the project area requires a higher degree of land management as per the SCIF indigenous People Policy.

191 World Bank (2018), Doing Business 2018, http://www.doingbusiness.org/en/reports/rankings/laos/index.html

# G

## Data gaps: Considerable forest-related data are available for the project area, but socio-economic data are available mostly only at provincial level and are based on sources such as the Population and Housing Census. The project will need to address these data gaps in order to identify potential environmental and social risks that will require addressing so that the project may work with a reasonable socio-economic baseline. Such a baseline is necessary for effective risk management, including risk and safeguard monitoring.

### G.1.2 Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP)

The ESMP is provided in Annex Eb. Designed in response to the risks identified in the ESIA, the purpose of the ESMP is to provide a practical plan to manage the environmental and social risks and impacts associated with the project. The ESMP will be developed by the Project Team, in close collaboration with the communities and other stakeholders affected by the project.

### G.1.3 Project Risk Mitigation Approach

The ESIA, being based on the guidance provided in the CGI’s Environmental and Social safeguards policy, adopted in March 2018, and the Indigenous Peoples Policy, and it has therefore extensively incorporated this guidance into its assessment of risks and impacts. The ESMP will be developed with reference to Environment and Social sustainability, the World Bank’s Safeguards Policy and the Lao national safeguards policy.

Performance Standard 1: Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts

ESIA observation: The project will require a suitable ESMP that is in place for its duration. It will need to establish a mitigation strategy for each identified risk, including those at provincial level. Further, it will have to establish acceptable and appropriate communications and redress mechanisms.

Project mitigation approach: A detailed ESMP has been developed (Annex Eb), accompanied by a stakeholder engagement plan (Annex Zb). The ESMP is based on the findings of the ESIA and includes a risk matrix and a knowledge management plan (Annex Zz). In order to inform project mitigation activities, the following surveys will be conducted:

- Baseline socio-economic data collection in all target village site clusters
- NTFP inventory, including specific surveys for site-specific areas that highlight what is available locally
- Land use survey, including specific surveys for sites that have high risk in site-specific areas
- Economic investment survey in site-specific areas, to uncover the extent to which large-scale economic investments are likely to impact on the project area and its communities
- District- and provincial-level resettlement and relocation surveys to uncover the extent to which villages may be affected by planned development projects (including villages that may lose access to land but will not have to move households building)
- District-level household, surveying assistance and level of district staff and volunteers, and ethnic language capabilities.
- Community surveys for housing and facilities and those who have access to these facilities actually use them
- A community survey for the construction of the institution and facilities and those who have access to these details about beneficiaries and government staff working on each project to aid coordination and avoid duplication.
- Updated provincial land use maps, some of which have not been updated since 1995.

**Page 90**

The Green Climate Fund Funding Proposal (V2.0) Page 86 of 95

# The project will:

- Ensure that pesticides/herbicides are used (legal) not on any national or international banned lists) – with particular attention to the use of herbicides on the list of banned pesticides in 2011 but is widely used in upland crop production in northern Laos.
- Ensure that DAFOs receive appropriate training on the laws and regulations governing pesticide/herbicide use and handling, including the prohibition of their use during pregnancy.
- Ensure that proper safety measures are taken when pesticides/herbicides are used: provision of awareness-raising and training.
- Promote environmentally-friendly value chains as part of the project’s work on deforestation-free agricultural value chains, raising the profile of (i) the benefits of the reduced use of pesticides/herbicides; and (ii) the potential financial and marketing advantages of adopting or stopping the use of pesticides/herbicides (e.g. through the use of alternative agricultural practices, marketing of organic products, etc.).

# The project will:

- Ensure that land access considerations – including gender, ethnic and income-differentiated risks – are fully incorporated into the development of Community Land Project Plans (CLPPs) developed by the project. Such considerations will include (non-exhaustive):
  - Consideration of the potential for CLPPs to provide a legal basis for communities’ ability to gather NTFPs for domestic or commercial use, and ensuring continued access to land, forest and water resources.
  - Respect for ancestral and spiritual land and forest use, and sensitivity to customary use of land by the community, including ethnic groups, and ensuring rights remain to conduct ceremonies (which often take place in forests).
  - Provision for respecting indigenous knowledge, including the knowledge of use of medicinal plants.
- Ensure that the project does not displace households by inclusion: LUPs do not lose access to land while not gaining access to irrigation facilities.
- Provide access to information in appropriate ethnic languages, at the very least providing a translator for meetings.

**Page 91**

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 87 OF 95

## Performance Standard 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

### ESIA observation:
There is a potential that some of the agricultural activities, especially promotion of agroforestry, will turn the Revegetated (RV) category of land to plantations, thus reducing biodiversity. Pesticide use would have negative impacts on biodiversity.

### Project mitigation approach: Regarding the use of pesticides/herbicides, see above. Performance Standard 4 mitigates this risk by:

- Identifying land that is suitable for new forest:
  - Stipulating that a combination of spatial planning and participatory land-use planning with villagers is a prerequisite to ensure that the project does not result in deforestation.
  - Ensuring that the use of GIS and ground-based data collection and analysis, including factors that determine suitability for forest planting, will be used to identify areas where new forests can be planted (e.g., areas that are suitable for tree farming).
- Stipulating that no primary natural forest on permanent agricultural land is used for forest plantation.
  - Ensuring that the project does not result in deforestation or conversion of forest land.
- Stipulating the types of new forest planted:
  - On land used for agriculture and forestry, at least 70% of the land will be allocated to industrial trees; 20% will be buffer zone and at least 10% of plantation tree species will be native to Laos.

### Performance Standard 5: Indigenous peoples

#### ESIA observation:
The project would impact more people from the non-Lao-Tai ethnic groups than from the Lao-Tai ethnic group in most of the selected districts.

#### Project mitigation approach: See above, Performance Standards 1 and 5. The project will minimize, mitigate and compensate for adverse impacts on indigenous peoples by:

- Developing Ethnic Group Development Plans based on the Ethnic Group Development Planning Framework to be implemented through the project.
- Application of FPIC prior to the implementation of project activities, maintained throughout the project's Mekong Alpine Forest Management Plan (MAFMP).
  - Ensuring that indigenous people have better access to land and technical support for the implementation of good agricultural practices, SFM and FLP.
- National laws will be respected by the project, and all project activities have been screened against the national legal framework.
- Project staff and trainers to include male and female representatives from diverse ethnic groups and positively engage with indigenous groups. Project staff will further receive training on gender equality and social inclusion within the context of the project.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 88 OF 95

* **Outcome:** Extension and technical support at the community level, workshops and capacity building activities shall be inclusive, culturally appropriate, and take into account local traditional knowledge.
* **All information on project activities will be made easily accessible, and in appropriate ethnic languages. Land use plans will be developed with communities and stakeholders involved as early as possible; translations can be made available if necessary.**
* **Objective:** To gather information with other stakeholders (e.g., CSOs, Lao Women’s Union, etc.) to be sought out to strengthen stakeholder outreach and the engagement of various ethnic groups and vulnerable households.
* **Performance Standard 8: Cultural Heritage**

**ESIA observation:** During project preparation and consultation, no cultural heritage buildings or monuments, or related artifacts were identified. If such heritage artifacts could be problematic, areas identified in the project area nonetheless, residual uncertainty remains. It is thus recommended that further investigation of cultural practices and places of worship be conducted to identify any potential risks associated with these activities and ensure they are not affected. Such a process will be participatory, closely coordinated with communities and local leaders to identify village areas of importance.

**Project mitigation approach:** The risk associated with Performance Standard 8 is assessed as medium. The following measures will support the project to manage this risk:
* **All information on project activities will be made easily accessible, and in appropriate ethnic languages. Land use plans will be developed with communities and stakeholders involved as early as possible; translations can be made available if necessary.**
* **Collaboration with local stakeholders will continue throughout the projects lifetime, as local stakeholder and community members will have a key role in the implementation and monitoring of the project. This will ensure that stakeholder feedback is collected on an ongoing basis. The involvement of women’s groups and CSOs will be used as an important mechanism for collecting ongoing feedback throughout project implementation.**
* **For cultural heritage sites, a baseline assessment will be conducted to identify any potential risks associated with land use change. A follow-up assessment will be conducted at the end of the project to assess whether the risk has been managed or mitigated.**

**Information sharing:** The involvement of local stakeholders and affected communities in decision-making processes will pay particular potential to women, ethnic groups, illiterate people, people with disabilities, and people with limited or no access to internet, among others. While this is not a requirement under the ESIA, it is recommended that the project team engage with these local actors, including village and subdistrict leaders, producer associations, CSOs, the Lao Women’s Union and others.

* **If objects of cultural heritage are uncovered by the project, the procedures described in the World Bank ESMF Annex 6b will be followed. These include conducting baseline assessment activities in the area, demarcating and securing the area, and notifying the respective members of the PPRB and responsible government authorities (including its Provincial Department of Culture) within 24 hours.**

## Gender Assessment

* **The Gender Assessment (GA) is provided in Annex Baa and the Gender Action Plan (GAP) is provided in Annex Bb.**
    * Women still face discrimination in many aspects of their lives despite government policy to promote gender equality and protect women’s and children’s rights.
    * The project design addresses the identified gender gaps (see Annex Baa for an extended list):
        + The current draft of the new Forestry Law and the PRAPs do not reflect the gender aspects of forestry. Future negotiations will be required to ensure that gender is considered as a crucial factor in forest protection, monitoring, equal user rights and benefit-sharing, as well as resource management.

#### 203. The Government of Lao PDR (represented by MAF and MORRE), as a Project 1 Executing Entity, will sign a subsidiary agreement with GIZ based on UN standard operating procedures for financing contracts. This financing contract will be used to provide technical assistance to the government institution under MAFRIS—to enable the EFP to manage and operate grant disbursements through the REEDD+ Fund—while also supporting its programming and addressing its activities in accordance with GIZ and its own standard operating procedures.

G.3.1 Financial Management

204\. The financial management of the project will follow GIZ internal rules and regulations. GIZ has bank accounts with Deutsche Bank and Commerzbank. GIZ will not open a specific bank account for the GCF project but will ensure that all funds provided are clearly identifiable and distinguishable from GIZ’s other funds by setting up appropriate accounting procedures.

205\. In case of procurement by GIZ, GIZ will follow its own procurement guidelines. GIZ is required to comply with the principles contracting as enshrined in the German Act against Restraints of Competition (GWB), the German Regulation on the Award of Contracts for Public Works and, if applicable, the Contracting Rules for the Award of Public Works Contracts. The rules apply to all procurements by GIZ under this project.

When awarding contracts for supplies and services (including consultancy services) to be financed in full or in part from GCF funds, GIZ will ensure that the contracting parties comply with the German Act against Restraints of Competition for procurement and will, in any case, comply with the GIZ minimum standards. An overview of these minimum standards is provided in the annexes to this document. The GIZ Procurement Unit will assess adherence of submitted procurement documents with GIZ procurement regulations at defined stages in the process.

G.3.2 Procurement

206\. Independent external auditors will perform annual financial audits of the project line in time with International Auditing Standards. GIZ will be responsible for selecting and engaging the external auditors.

G.4 Disclosure of funding proposal

No disclosure of the funding proposal by an accredited entity confirms that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may be disclosed in full to the GCF, as no information is being provided in confidence.

□ With confidential information: The accredited entity declares that the funding proposal, including its annexes, may not be disclosed in full to the GCF, as certain information is being provided in confidence. Accordingly, the accredited entity will provide to the GCF two copies of the funding proposal:

- A full copy for internal use of the GCF in which the confidential portions are marked accordingly, together with an explanation regarding the nature and scope of the corresponding reason for confidentiality under the accredited entity’s disclosure policy; and
- Redacted copy for disclosure on the GCF website.

The funding proposal can only be processed upon receipt of the two copies above, including confidential information.

Vientiane, 25 February 2019

To: The Green Climate Fund (GCF)

Subject: No-Objection Letter - Funding proposal for the GCF by GIZ regarding “Implementation of the Lao PDR Emission Reductions Programme through improved governance and sustainable forest landscape management”

Dear Madam, Sir,

We refer to the project "Implementation of the Lao PDR Emission Reductions Programme through improved governance and sustainable forest landscape management" as included in the funding proposal submitted by GIZ to us on 12th February 2019.

The undersigned is the duly authorized of the National Designated Authority of Lao PDR.

Pursuant to GCF decision B(08)10, the content of which we acknowledge to have reviewed, we hereby communicate our no-objection to the project “Implementation of the Lao PDR Emission Reductions Programme through improved governance and sustainable forest landscape management” as included in the funding proposal.

By communicating our no-objection, it is implied that:

(a) The government of Lao PDR has no objection to the project as included in the funding proposal;
(b) The project, as included in the funding proposal, is in conformity with Lao PDR’s national policies, strategies and plans; 
(c) In accordance with the GCF’s environmental and social safeguards, the project as included in the funding proposal is in conformity with relevant national laws and regulations.

We also confirm that our national process for ascertaining no-objection to the project “Implementation of the Lao PDR Emission Reductions Programme through improved governance and sustainable forest landscape management” as included in the funding proposal has been duly followed. 

We also confirm that our no-objection applies to all projects or activities to be implemented within the scope of the programme.

We acknowledge that this letter will be made publicly available on the GCF website.

Sincerely,

Syamphone Sengchoukanda
National Designated Authority of Lao PDR

Dear Mr. Syamphone Sengchandala, Deputy Director General Department of Climate Change,

National Designated Authority (NDA) to the Green Climate Fund (GCF),

Ref: Request for no-objection for the Funding Proposal to be submitted to the GCF by GIZ regarding the project 'Implementation of the Lao PDR Emission Reductions Programme through improved governance and sustainable forest landscape management'

As having central roles for the implementation of the Lao PDR's Emission Reduction Programme Document (ERPD), Department of Forestry, Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry herewith request a full cooperation from your Department to submit a No- Objection Letter for Funding Proposal of the Lao PDR Emission Reductions Programme through improved governance and sustainable forest landscape management' to be submitted to the GCF by GIZ as Accredited Entity by 28th February 2018.

The funding proposal was jointly developed by Department of Forestry (DOF) and GIZ in close collaboration with various stakeholders. The project is based on experiences in a consultative manner and in close cooperation with various co-financing partners, including ADB, JICA, BMZ and IFAD. More importantly, the funding proposal has a commitment to cover the watershed management component of the ADB project 'Sustainable Rural Infrastructure and Watershed Management'.

It is important to highlight at this point that the project has already been included into the GCF country pipeline in 2017. Besides, the project has high priority for the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry (MAF) to leverage future result-based payments of approximately USD 42 million under the REDD+ Strategy until 2035, which is expected to be implemented June 2018.

With the Emission Reductions Payment Agreement to be signed in January 2020, the country has the opportunity to reduce emissions from deforestation and forest degradation over a period of 5 years and at this time period the proposed GCF project is the main funding source to implement the National REDD+ Strategy, the Emission Reductions Program and the Provincial REDD+ Action Plan.

Furthermore, the funding proposal is in conformity with Lao PDR's national priorities, strategies and plans. It significantly contributes to the mitigation target of the Nationally

Determined Contributions (NDCs), the NLEDP targets in terms of reducing deforestation and increasing the national forest cover. Therefore, it is of utmost importance for MAF to prioritize this proposal and bring it to the 23rd GCF Board Meeting in July 2019.

For this reason, we would like to request your kind consideration to notify a no-objection for this funding proposal to GCF Secretariat in due time.

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |

It is important to ensure that adequate financing is necessary for the successful implementation of project activities. |
| Its programmatic context allows for a strong linkage with the existing Lao PDR FCPF Emissions Reduction Programme in the Lao People’s Democratic Republic, which complements and supports the World Bank, which the proposal complements and supports. | The incentives sharing/payment should ensure the adequate provision of incentives to all types of stakeholders including communities, village development funds, civil society organizations and small and medium-sized enterprises. |

This is a form of private sector and the Government of the Lao People’s Democratic Republic (with co-financing ratio of 3:2). |  |

The project will result in 4.8 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2eq) of emission reductions over the four-year project implementation period. This equates to a mitigation cost to the GCF of US$ 2.75 per t CO2eq.

**Beneficiaries:** The project area is given as the most vulnerable and poorest regions of the Lao People's Democratic Republic.


The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions outlined in annexes A and B, and addendum XVI, titled 'List of proposed conditions and recommendations'.

## Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### 2.1 Project background

This project proposal consists of a stand-alone project (Project 1) embedded in a programmatic context. Two additional projects sharing a similar theory of change will be developed under the same programmatic content and will be submitted for Board approval at a future date. Board approval of this project is intended to be wholly separate from, and does not pre-judge, Board approval for future related projects.

This project is expected to run from mid-2020 to mid-2024 and will be implemented in the three provinces of Luang Prabang, Xieng Khouang, and Bokeo, which have the highest rates of deforestation and forest degradation within the programme area.


The Lao People's Democratic Republic is a landlocked least developed country (LLDC) in South-East Asia. The country has a population of approximately 7 million people, with agriculture, electricity generation (especially hydropower), and mining as key economic sectors. Agriculture, forestry and fisheries contribute around US$1 billion per year to the economy, or about 40 percent of the Lao workforce.

Forest cover declined from 50 per cent of the country’s surface area in the mid-1960s to just over thirty-five per cent by the mid-1990s. In the last fifteen years, forest cover has amounted to approximately 680,000 hectares (ha). Moreover, the situation regarding forest degradation and deforestation is a major source of carbon stock changes in biodiversity and livelihoods – in as concerning as deforestation.

The net annual average greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions from land-use change and forestry activities are estimated at approximately 12.5 million tonnes of CO2 equivalent (tCO2eq). Overall, the forestry and land-use change sector is responsible for 67 per cent of emission reductions in the country’s economy, while agriculture accounts for another 28 per cent. Nevertheless, the Government of the Lao People's Democratic Republic has recently introduced a new national forest policy that aims to achieve the following targets: (i) 70 per cent forest cover; (ii) a national REDD+ plus strategy; (iii) a timber export ban and a new forest law.

The project provides a strategic opportunity to achieve a paradigm shift in the forest and land-use sector of the Lao People's Democratic Republic while mitigating GHG emissions from the forest sector. The Lao People's Democratic Republic has embraced REDD+ plus as a way to address its principal source of GHG emissions.


---
**Page 107**
---

## Climate Objective

### Mitigation

For the last 10 years, the Lao People’s Democratic Republic has progressed substantially towards REDD+ and related reforms, notably through the acceptance of the forest reference (emission) level, the finalised REDD+ Strategy, the strategic and social environmental assessment, the development of a national carbon accounting system for forests and land use as well as the final acceptance of the Emission Reductions Programme Document with the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF). Consequently, in 2019, the Lao People’s Democratic Republic is now fully prepared to be advanced for Phase 2 of REDD+ implementation in order to reduce forest-sector emissions and achieve true paradigm shift which means considering forest landscapes as a whole and not just forests alone. This project will contribute towards this goal.

Although the project is a mitigation initiative, the Lao People’s Democratic Republic considers itself a least developed country (LDC) with limited adaptive capacities that it is highly vulnerable to climate change impacts. Indeed, forestry and agriculture are two of four key sectors identified in the national adaptation plan as being most vulnerable to the consequences of action, as well as five key sectors identified in the nationally determined contribution as being highly vulnerable to the consequences of action. These include: (i) vulnerability to risk of river flooding; landslides, cyclones and wildfires; a high risk for extreme heat; and a low risk for droughts where there are specific climate change impacts identified for the regions to be targeted with this project.

After decades of losing tropical forest, the Lao People’s Democratic Republic is actively seeking solutions to reverse this trend. The country has demonstrated its commitment by committing substantial domestic resources and having worked closely with bilateral and multilateral partners in order to develop a comprehensive approach to REDD+ implementation with complementary initiatives. The Lao People’s Democratic Republic will deploy GCF support to remove investment barriers to unlock forest-related payments, domestic taxes and fees and create a sustainable financing mechanism for the upscaling of REDD+.

The project is expected to extend the expected paradigm shift in the forest and land-use sector of the Lao People’s Democratic Republic while mitigating GHGs from the forest sector by:

(a) Strengthening the enabling environment for a REDD-plus implementation (REDD-plus standards, monitoring and reporting systems, including a monitoring, reporting and verification (MRV) system for REDD-plus);

(b) Promoting market solutions for agricultural drivers of deforestation (deforestation-free certification schemes, agroforestry, agroforestry+), and

(c) Supporting climate change mitigation action through forestry (sustainable forest landscapes).

The project is fully aligned with the Lao People’s Democratic Republic GCF Country Programme (updated in February 2019), which identifies REDD-plus as one of four mitigation priorities, and the increase and maintenance of forest cover as one of five short-term priorities.

The project will contribute to the following Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs) in the results area: (i) Scaling up REDD-plus action; (ii) Strengthening Sustainable Forest Landscapes.

The executing entities (EEs) for the project are the Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ), Germany, and the Lao People’s Democratic Republic (represented by the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry (MAF) and the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment), and Japan International Cooperation Agency (JICA).

### 1.1.6 Climate Objective

Mitigation


---
**Page 108**
---

1\. The project will result in 5.6 million tCO2eq of emission reductions over the four-year project implementation period in three regions of the Lao People's Democratic Republic: Houaphan, Sayaboury and Luang Prabang. The emission reductions will occur by:

(a) Reducing emissions from deforestation (a reduction in deforestation of 6 per cent, equivalent to 105,840 ha).

(b) Reducing emissions from forest degradation (a reduction of forest degradation of 10 per cent, equivalent to 39,720 ha), and

(c) Increasing carbon stocks in forests (an increase of reforestation and afforestation of 6 per cent and 1 per cent, respectively, equivalent to 3,168.1 ha of forest restoration and 1,850 ha of reforestation).

(d) These combined emissions reductions equate to a reduction of 6 per cent compared to the CFCCP Reference Level emission and an increase in removals equivalent to 975,000 tCO2eq, which is an increase of 4 per cent compared to the removals in the Reference Level.

Adaptation

1\. Although the project is mitigation only (DESD plus), it will serve to address climate vulnerabilities in the Lao People's Democratic Republic, thus generating adaptation co-benefits. However, the accredited entity (AE) has elected not to claim adaptation co-benefits for this project.

2\. The Lao People's Democratic Republic identifies itself as an LDC with limited adaptive capacities and it has been ranked as one of the most climate-vulnerable countries in the region. At a national level, the country experienced significant climatic events during the period 1966-2005, including floods (10 per cent), storms (14 per cent) and drought (14 per cent). In addition to these extreme weather events, the projected climate change impacts for the project implementation region(s), a temperature increase of 0.8–0.9°C relative to the historical average is expected by 2050 (with an upper bound increase slightly higher [11-15°C above the average] but with greater temporal variability).

3\. The project responds to observed and expected climate change impacts through different adaptation strategies, including: improving water management practices and supporting dry-season irrigation schemes; providing training on sustainable water harvesting techniques; programme development for improved livelihoods; institutional capacity building; monitoring; and other aspects described in detail in the funding proposal.

2.1.3 Financing

2\. The AE has requested that the project be financed by a EUR 15.2 million grant from GCF, with total co-financing of EUR 58.0 million. The AE requires GCF cover 23.2 per cent of the total project cost (EUR 4.6 million) and co-financing partners to contribute the remaining amount.

2\. The Lao People's Democratic Republic (EUR 11.2 million), the German Federal Ministry for Economic Cooperation and Development (BMZ) (EUR 5.0 million), the United Nations Development Programme (UNDP) (EUR 3.8 million), the Asian Development Bank (ADB) (EUR 12.4 million), the International Fund for Agricultural Development (IFAD) (EUR 7.7 million) and the World Bank (EUR 6.5 million).

2\. The AE has estimated a co-financing ratio of 3:2 for the four-year project.

2\. The project is expected to be implemented as part of a larger, 9.5-year programme, which will cover all six provinces in the northern area of the Lao People's Democratic Republic that are covered by the Emissions Reduction (ER) Programme. The larger programme covers the same six provinces in the northern area of the Lao People's Democratic Republic that comprise the ER Programme area. However, this 9.5-year project addresses the three provinces of Houaphan, Sayaboury and Luang Prabang.

### 2.1.3 Environmental and social safeguards

#### 24. The All has classified the entire programme, and thus the project, as category B.

##### 25. The project will be implemented in a manner that meets the requirements of various policies and standards, including those of the GCF, the AIE, and its implementing partners. The ESMA provides a detailed gaps assessment of these policies and standards. Notwithstanding this, it may still apply to more stringent requirements than those required by the AIE, the country, or the GCF.

##### 26. The environmental and social impact assessment (ESIA) presented an exclusion list. The list identifies activities that may raise the overall environmental and social risk profile of the project activity. These include land use change, conversion of primary forests, absence of consent from indigenous peoples, and physical displacement of communities. If these activities are implemented, they will be considered sensitive and will limit the environmental and social risk category of the project activities to "moderate".

##### 27. The ESIA identified several latent positive as well as negative environmental and social impacts, which are described in section III of this document.

#### 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

##### 28. The project aims to catalyze a shift in land use, land-use change and forestry and establish sustainable forest management practices for the long-term conservation of forests and landscapes. The project will have four outputs: (1) creation of enabling environment for REDD+ implementation; (2) market development and capacity building for REDD+ through carbon finance and through forestry; and (4) project management, coordination, monitoring and reporting.

##### 29. These outputs are components of the project that will be implemented in parallel to support the overall objective of the project: strengthening the capacities of stakeholders; support for integrated multi-level planning; strengthening of enforcement mechanisms; promotion of sustainable forest management practices and agroforestry; a private sector value chain; investments in rural infrastructure and watershed management; village forest management; and sustainable forest management, including community-based forest management.

##### Component 1: Enabling environment for REDD+ plus implementation (total cost: EUR 8.0 million, GCF cost: EUR 3.65 million)

###### The project activities will enhance the availability of financing for a major transformation in the way the Lao People's Democratic Republic manages its forest landscapes, including by supporting the development and strengthening of an enabling environment; developing a new regulatory framework for forestry; and improving the enforcement of the new regulatory framework.

###### 30. The primary focus is to unlock GHG-emission reductions stemming from the project that will be achieved through the implementation of the REDD+ plus component, which includes the LAF and MAF of the Lao People's Democratic Republic in exchange for results-based payments amounting to EUR 15 million per year (which are flowing into the Environmental Protection Fund (EPF) for the first four-year period).

###### 31. The establishment of the REDD+ plus funding window would offer significant benefits for blending international, national, public and private sources of finance; ensuring funds are


---
**Page 110**
---

**Component 2: Market solutions for agricultural drivers of deforestation (total cost EUR 308 million; GCF ID: GCFF/24/RP/016)**

This component will support deforestation-free agriculture and agroforestry by enhancing productivity, increasing farmers' integration into agricultural value chains, and improving the economic viability of forest-based livelihoods. It will also help to reduce pressure on forests.

The component consists of three activities relating to the promotion of good agricultural practices (GAP) in key agricultural commodities, reducing deforestation risk through supply chain expansion pressure into forested landscapes.


---
**Page 111**
---

Activities under this component build on, among other things, analytical work being undertaken by the Food and Agriculture Organization of the United Nations through the GCF Readiness project for REDD-plus. This work has demonstrated that the Lao People’s Democratic Republic (LPDR) has a large number of forest landscapes with high conservation value and provide additional incentives to reduce pressure on existing forest areas (directly through land-use practices, and through the creation of additional income-generating activities and employment opportunities).

This component will support activities specifically designed to curb expansion into forested lands, while also promoting sustainable management of production forests exposed to climate risks. Activities are also expected to mobilize private sector investment in agroforestry, and detailed planning will ensure that agroforestry activities are implemented in suitable areas and in a manner consistent with the overall landscape approach.

Component 2 will value chain analysis to identify linkages, or the absence thereof, between forest landscapes and production forests. This will help to identify potential gaps, potential efficiency and profitability gains, and thereby contribute to inclusive value chain competitiveness. Specifically, value chain analysis at the provincial level will analyze existing as well as new deforestation-free value chains. The analysis will also identify opportunities for new deforestation-free value chains will be promoted.

Component 2: Forest landscape management and sustainable forest management is expected under the planned ADB project Sustainable Rural Infrastructure and Watershed Management Sector, which has been designed in conjunction with the project and whose principal objective is to secure long-term benefits from forests. The component will complement this project by aligning with the ADB project and well ensure that the forested landscapes in the catchment areas remain intact and are not converted into agricultural land. This will help reduce the risk of deforestation and degradation by addressing the following key underlying causes: lack of alternative livelihoods, poor access to markets, low agricultural productivity, lack of value-added activities and weak negotiation/marshalling skills.

Component 3: Climate change mitigation actions through forestry (total cost: EUR 12.3 million; GCF core fund contribution: EUR 9.85 million).

Component 3 will build upon output 1 (enabling environment) and will reduce emissions through sustainable forest landscape management and the promotion of forest landscapes in the three categories where village forest management is to be implemented.

Village forest management will be implemented in all three forest categories (production forests, conservation forests, and mixed-purpose forests), including production forest and conservation forest as well as in village forest following a landscape approach. In total, there is high carbon-stock forest area of 2.3 million ha in the three provinces where village forest management is to be implemented. The majority of the project implementation focuses their on-the-ground activities; village forest management can be supported in a total of 1.5 million ha.

This component will promote the sustainable management of production forests. There are 51 production forest areas in the Lao People’s Democratic Republic, and the scale of deforestation is high (46,500 ha) as a % more or less satisfactory quality. In the project province of Luang Namtha, the total area under production forest management accounts for 0.3 million ha and agricultural land for 32,000 ha, which could be used for restoration and reforestation. Moreover, there is a logging ban in place in natural forests

1 Deformation-free value chains are those that can demonstrate that deforestation has not occurred throughout the value chain from primary production through to sale to the consumer.

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High

48. The project has a clear climate rationale. The project is expected to reduce emissions by 5.6 million tCO2e (an average of 1.4 million tCO2eq/annum), consisting of 4.8 million tCO2e in emission reductions and 0.8 million tCO2e in carbon sequestration. This corresponds to a reduction of 9 per cent compared to the PCFP Reference Level emissions; and increase removals by 13 per cent, equivalent to 576 ha of forest restoration and reforestation to the removals in the Reference Level. The funding proposal has high impact potential.

49. As a result of the direct and the enabling implementation activities, over its four-year implementation period, the project is expected to achieve an average annual reduction of deforestation of 5 per cent, equivalent to 9,900 ha; (ii) a reduction of forest degradation of 13 per cent, equivalent to 27,850 ha; and (iii) an increase in reforestation and reforestation of 6 per cent and 1 per cent, respectively - equivalent to 3,160 ha of forest restoration and 1,550 ha of reforestation.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

50. The project, during its four-year implementation period, is expected to unlock REDD+ results-based payments of EUR 23 million from the RFK Carbon Fund. The project provides a model for scaling up and replicating forest restoration and reforestation in the land-use sector of the Lao People’s Democratic Republic while mitigating GHG from the forest sector. The project will also contribute to the country’s efforts to reduce its principal source of GHG emissions and proposes innovative institutional and financial arrangements (e.g., carbon finance, value addition, carbon-plus agriculture supply chains) to ensure the long-term sustainability of the investments.

51. The identified elements driving a paradigm shift - scale-up and replication, knowledge and learning, and policy development - are expected to influence regional frameworks and policies – will apply at both the project and programme levels. At the programme level, the project is expected to contribute to the sustainable management of forests in the provinces of the Lao People’s Democratic Republic as well as in neighbouring countries with similar landscape/forestry contexts.


---
**Page 113**
---

10. The project will enable a paradigm shift across different sectors and business activities: agricultural, industrial, and financial sectors in particular will be targeted as key sectors for transformation. Within these sectors, the project will work throughout the agricultural and forestry value chains. In addition to the direct beneficiaries of the project, the program target will target sectors and commodities where there is strong and consistent demand in order to ensure the profitability of the supported sectors.

12. The project will be implemented on a phased basis, which will serve as a model for the rest of the country. The project will generate opportunities for leveraging additional private sector funding through public-private partnerships (PPPs). This will include increasing investments into rural northern areas of the Lao People’s Democratic Republic and scaling up to the entire northern region. The project will also seek to leverage existing REDD+ funds (the EEP and the Lao Forest and Resource Development Fund (LFRDF)), the project is expected to facilitate replication and upscaling as well as the broader distribution of REDD-plus finance at scale, including through the creation of a national REDD-plus fund and managing funds for REDD-plus.

14. Also, the iterative learning approach of the project under a programmatic context provides the opportunity to gather data and learn iteratively for more effective and adaptive design. It also offers the potential for crowding in substantial sustainable financing from diversified sources (e.g., private sector, international development partners, larger endowment for the REDD-plus funding window), thereby ensuring long-term financial sustainability.

3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

The project targets 120,000 direct beneficiaries and 135,000 indirect beneficiaries. Through the conversion of rice cultivation to more forest-friendly approaches, the project provides a just transition by creating better incomes, jobs and skills. Among other benefits, these will contribute to an increase in food security and improved nutrition.

The economic net present value attributed to the local beneficiary is estimated as EUR 312 million for the whole programme.


The project has more significant potential for contributing positively to gender equality and will work closely with the Lao Women’s Union to ensure the effective engagement of women. Moreover, special needs of indigenous groups and indigenous groups will be addressed by the project in several ways as described in the proposal.

57. With regard to the economic pillar, the project is expected to improve the livelihoods and incomes of the local population through increased agricultural productivity and strengthen domestic resource mobilization. As for the social pillar, the project will contribute to reducing hunger and improving nutrition and health through improved agriculture production, better access to markets, and improved food security among vulnerable groups; and improving gender equality through economic training and entrepreneurial activities.

Finally, the environmental pillar of the project is expected to contribute to the sustainable forest sector and increase carbon sequestration from the atmosphere and contribute to the sustainable management and efficient use of natural resources (notably, forests and land).

3.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

58. The Lao People’s Democratic Republic is an LDC rated as ranked 139 out of 189 on the Human Development Index; this is the western area of the Lao People’s Democratic Republic historically been the poorest and most rural region of the country. The average annual income


---
**Page 114**
---

in the project area is approximately USD 1,200 per capita compared with a national average of USD 2,378.

59 Although the project is a mitigation (REDD-plus) initiative, it will serve to address climate vulnerabilities in the Lao People's Democratic Republic. The Lao People's Democratic Republic has been identified as being particularly vulnerable to climate change impacts, and the project responds to climate change impacts in a number of ways.

60 The project seeks to remove barriers that currently prevent Lao stakeholders from accessing climate and REDD-plus finance to help address their financial, economic, social and institutional needs.

### 3.5 Country ownership
**Scale: High**

61 The proposed project is aligned with and directly delivers on the priorities outlined by the Government's national development, climate change, forest and agriculture strategies.

62 The programme has formed part of the GCF project pipeline for the Lao People's Democratic Republic since 2017 and has been formally prioritised by both MoF and the national designated authority (NDA) as a priority under the National Strategic Development Plan (NSDP).

63 The experience and track-record of the AE (GEZ) and the three EEAs demonstrate a capacity to deliver.

64 GEZ is currently undergoing accreditation. As such, the experience they will get from the project is unique, and they can later channel future funds through the REDD-plus funding window and further contribute to scaling up.

### 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness
**Scale: Medium-High**

65 The project will result in 5.6 million tCO2eq of emission reductions over the four-year project implementation period. This equates to a mitigation cost to the GCF of EUR 17,290 per tCO2eq.

66 The co-financing ratio is 3:2 (GCF budget: EUR 15,160,596; and co-financing: EUR 840,308,500). The finance leveraged by the project is estimated to be EUR 227 million.

67 The programme will not rely on any single financier for funding. Instead, it will focus its financing on specific activities. It is understood that each financier is financing the activities that best fit their expertise and interest.

#### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards
68 Environmental and social risk category: The AE has classified the entire programme as category B following the definition of environmental and social risk category of the AE and GCF, indicating that the likely risks and impacts from the activities are limited, largely reversible and readily mitigated. The assessment by GEZ confirms the category B classification of the programme.

69 The project will contribute to the achievement in Lao PDR of enabling conditions and establish viable models for the management of forestry and landscapes. The project will have three outputs: (1) creation of an enabling environment for REDD-plus implementation; (2)

market drivers for agricultural drivers of deforestation, and (3) climate change mitigation through forests. The project also includes activities related to management, coordination, monitoring and reporting. These outputs are composed of closely interrelated activities, including: development of a strategic environmental assessment (SEA); national level planning; strengthening of enforcement and monitoring; knowledge management; investments in good agricultural practices and agroforestry; sector wide support for rural infrastructure and water resource management; village forest management; and sustainable forest management including productive forests.

### 70. **Strategic Environmental Assessment (SEA)**

The SEA discloses the AE. The AE developed an EIA and environmental and social management plan (ESM) covering the entire programme, including project activities, the SESA, and the ESIA for the Enterprise Development Group. Planning framework and a detailed stakeholder engagement over the environmental and social due diligence by the AE is considered comprehensive. The various environmental and social risks and impacts of the programme are identified in the EIA, SEA, ESM, and impacts following the mitigation hierarchy. Supporting the EISA and ESMA are the SESA, ESM, and EIA. The EIA was developed for the entire project area under the Republic's Emissions Reduction Programme. While the SESA was developed for a larger scale set of activities in the project area, the EIA, SEA, ESM, and EIA were developed for a similar process and integrated recommendations from the SESA.

### 71. **Key environmental and social risks and impacts and mitigation measures**

The ESIA provides an overall assessment of the programme's environmental and social profile. The list includes activities that are considered to have significant positive or negative environmental and social impacts, such as those related to land tenure rights, conversion of primary forests, absence of consent from indigenous peoples and physical displacement affecting others. The list of excluded activities is limited in scope and does not limit the environmental and social categorisation of the programme activities to "moderate".

### 72. **Key environmental and social risks**

The ESIA identifies positive as well as negative environmental and social impacts, which are described in the following paragraphs:

(a) **Labour and working conditions** The SEA identified significant risks related to occupational health and safety for workers and stakeholders directly involved in the programme. Workers involved in forest management and operations can be exposed to potential hazards such as falls from trees, cuts from sharp tools, exposure to cutting and other forest management activities. Workers and programme staff will be trained in occupational health and safety practices and will be used appropriate personal protective equipment (PPE) where necessary. The AE has identified child labour in programme activities. Local labour, including women and members of ethnic groups are not excluded from working on the project.

(b) **Community health, safety and security** The activities and the infrastructure to be built will pose potential health and safety risks to the communities. In supporting the agroforestry component of the project, the use of agricultural chemicals such as fertilizers, pesticides and herbicides, among others. If not managed well, the use of agricultural chemicals can have negative impacts on human health and the environment in communities. The programme will not support the procurement of agrochemicals nor actively promote their use. However, the AE has identified that there is a ban on banned agrochemicals in the country, which are mostly used in plantations. A pesticide management plan will be developed to ensure safe handling and disposal of pesticide management measures for risks resulting from the use of agricultural chemicals. Such measures include prohibition or exclusion from programme support, training, guidance on the management of pesticides, and other risk reduction measures.

(c) **Additional risks** The additional risk from unexploded ordnance is also identified in the ESIA. Measures to


---
**Page 116**

# Management of Unexploded Ordnance Risks

The ESIA (Environmental and Social Impact Assessment) outlines established national legislation and practices for managing unexploded ordnance risks.

## Land Acquisition and Involuntary Resettlement

The programme does not anticipate any acquisition of lands or involuntary resettlement of households. However, activities related to the management of unexploded ordnance may result in restrictions on access to lands and resources. Such restrictions can lead to loss of livelihood and further displacement if they are not adequately managed.

## Biodiversity and Natural Resources

The programme will undertake biodiversity conservation activities within degraded lands. It will not support activities that could lead to the conversion of habitats or loss of biodiversity, such as the planting of a timber plantation. The use of agrochemicals may have negative impacts on biodiversity; mitigation measures include awareness-raising and capacity-building.

## Indigenous Peoples

The Government of the Lao People's Democratic Republic recognises the diversity of ethnic groups within the programme area, which includes diverse linguistic groups. Three major groups are identified: Tai-Lai, Non-Mon, and Khmu. The programme aims to address issues arising from activities, including displacement and tenure on traditional lands under customary use.

## Mitigation Measures

The programme will not interfere with the ethnic group's use of traditional knowledge and practices related to land management. It will also ensure that restrictions on access to lands and resources do not affect the groups' livelihoods or cultural practices.

# Conclusion

The Lao Front for National Development maintains representation in key programme decision-making structures, ensuring community involvement. The programme aims to promote voluntary participation from communities while maintaining support for those who may be affected.

75. Grievance redress mechanism. A programme-level grievance redress mechanism (GRM) will be established as part of the grievance redress system developed for REEDD plus in the country; it will be supported by a reporting mechanism to the various programme management units (PMUs). The PMU is the level closest to the communities affected and has the capacity to receive, assess and resolve grievances. The PMU will also report on processes from the district to the national level, where the grievances are expected to be resolved and communicated back to the affected party. The ESIA describes the programme-level GRM process in detail. The PMUs will ensure that the GRM is consistent with the customary dispute resolution practices of ethnic and religious groups, ensuring that the responses are culturally appropriate and sensitive to the needs of all stakeholders involved. Information on programme GRM will be included in the programme consultations and information sessions.

The NPMU will also be responsible for coordinating with the REEDD plus Task Force. ### 4.2 Gender policy

The AE has submitted a gender assessment and therefore complies with the operational guidelines of the GCF Gender Policy and Action Plan. The gender assessment was undertaken with a detailed review of existing literature, including the results from previous assessments on existing assessments in the forestry sector, and consultations with relevant national authorities and villagers in three villages in Houaphan and Luang Namtha, two of the provinces being targeted by the ESMP. The assessment also reviewed the role of women in decision-making in the community and in the sector of intervention. The gender assessment discusses the social stereotypes that exist regarding the roles of men and women in forestry management, communication, decision-making and leadership, education and economic participation. Women have significant roles in forest resource management at all levels, including as collectors, processors, transporters, and traders. However, they are often excluded from access to information on forest resources and finance. When they do have rights to forest products, they tend to be restricted to products that are not profitable or have lower commercial benefits. The assessment provides a number of implications for the ESMP in terms of how it can address gender issues. It also discusses ways to address the needs and priorities of women and men that were identified in the analysis, and describes how these will be addressed through stakeholder consultations which were included under action plan of the programme.

The AE has provided a project-level gender action plan. The plan has translated the recommendations from the gender assessment into actions that can be implemented together with the project to an effort to ensure access to benefits and participation in decision-making for women and men, including by setting sex-disaggregated targets where it is available, sex-disaggregated targets for some indicators, timelines for implementation of activities, and a monitoring framework. The ESMP does not have sex-disaggregated targets, and a baseline survey will be undertaken in the first year of implementation to collect data on the gender composition of the target population. Baseline data is collected before the programme commences implementation in its first year and use the baseline data to rationalize targets that were set in the gender action plan and the logic framework. The ESMP also includes a monitoring framework for tracking the number of beneficiaries of the programme and the number of beneficiaries relative to the total population disaggregated by sex.

The project intends to contribute to ensuring the following: (i) access to information through training to build practical business skills for women; (ii) capacity-building for government officials, CSOs, and other stakeholders in gender-sensitive approaches to effective implementation of programme activities in village communities; (iii) empowerment of women and men by providing them with opportunities to participate as members of project committees and in consultations related to investments in community-based agroforestry.

It is recommended that the AE review the existing gender action plan and ensure its implementation in all patrol groups. Access to finance will be supported by the provision of grants from the REDD+ financial mechanism (FCF) to implement community-based micro-action plans, including activities at the village level. The AE is advised to include, under the gender action plan, access to micro-grants for women and men to address the limited access to finance noted in the gender assessment.

The AE is advised to go beyond collecting information on the number of participants in the different training sessions and collect data on their knowledge and skills gained from the use of business skills acquired from training that will be provided as part of the project. Implementation arrangements include the recruitment, for the NMG of a safeguard gender specialist and a gender focal person. The gender specialist and gender expert will support the specialist in overseeing the mainstreaming of gender and implementation of the gender action plan. The AE is advised to ensure that the gender focal persons are women who will receive technical gender training to effectively integrate gender into the project.

### 4.3 Risks

#### 4.3.1 Overall project assessment (medium risk):

The GCF is requested to provide a grant of EUR 15.2 million to strengthen the enabling environment for REDD+ plus. This financing will help the country to earn Euro REDD+ results-based payments from FCPF of approximately EUR 23 million. While the benefit-sharing plan is being prepared, the AE should ensure that the project is not implemented before the indicative breakdown, the country will use most of the payment received from FCPF for local REDD+ projects.

The project has co-financing by way of a grant from BMZ, the Lao People's Democratic Republic Government (in-kind), JICA, a loan from ADB and IARL, and equity from the private sector. The AE is advised to ensure that the co-financing partners are aware that the GCF, BMZ and IARL will not be administered by GIZ; rather, it will be managed by the respective institutions in accordance with their own rules and regulations. The AE should also ensure that timely availability of total co-finance is necessary for the successful implementation of the project.

#### 4.3.2 AE's capability to execute the current project (medium risk):

The GIZ head office in Germany will be the AE and the GIZ office in the Lao People's Democratic Republic will serve as one of the EEs together with the Government of the Lao People's Democratic Republic (represented by MAF) and the Ministry of Natural Resources and Environment. The GIZ has been operating in the Lao People's Democratic Republic since 1993 and has a team of approximately 250 staff in the country.

In the proposed project, FCPF will channel EUR 10.2 million from a GCF grant to sub-beneficiaries. The Government of the Lao People's Democratic Republic will sign an

### 4.6 Legal assessment

108. The Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA) was signed with the Accredited Entity on 15 November 2017, and it became effective on 31 January 2019.

109. The Accredited Entity has provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.

110. The project is implemented in the People's Democratic Republic of Laos (PDR), a country in which the GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, according to international law, the GCF may be subject to criminal jurisdiction by this country, which risks need to be further assessed. The GCF Secretariat provided the NHA of Lao PDR with a draft privileges and immunities agreement on 21 February 2019. No response on the draft privileges and immunities agreement was received.

111. The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit (IIU) are responsible for reviewing any complaints or allegations related to project activities and/or investigations, where appropriate, in countries where GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the GCF are made only after a review of such complaints or allegations has been completed and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.
