# Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP117

## Proposal name:
Implementation of the Lao PDR Emission Reductions Programme through improved governance and sustainable forest management.

## Accredited entity:
Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit GmbH (GIZ)

## Project/programme size:
Medium

### Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel
#### 1. Impact potential: Scale: High

1. The Lao People's Democratic Republic is a landlocked least developed country (LLSC) in South-East Asia, where the economy is dependent on natural resources, especially forestry, agriculture, sources of electricity generation (especially hydropower) and mining. Agriculture, forestry and fisheries together account for 30% of GDP and directly or indirectly employ 64 per cent of its workforce.

2. In the last decade, Lao People's Democratic Republic has lost approximately 680,000 ha of forests. Forest cover accounts for 58 per cent of the country’s surface area, with a significant reduction in forest stocking owing to the valuable wood having been taken out, diminishing the value of the remaining trees and leading to their degradation.

3. The Lao People's Democratic Republic has committed itself to implement an ambitious REDD+ plus emission reductions programme for, which substantial domestic resources will be mobilized from various sources, including financial resources, to various projects. However, there are still immense challenges, including capacity and funding constraints, as well as the need to address existing governance issues.

4. The Lao People's Democratic Republic will deploy GCF support to remove investment barriers and thus enable access to concessional loans, domestic taxes and fees, and to create a sustainable environment for scaling-up REDD+.

5. This proposal consists of a stand-alone project (project 1) embedded in a programmed context of action (PCA) (project 2) and a second output (project 3), which will be submitted in the future for Board approval. It will be implemented in the previous three years, during which time deforestation rates have been among the highest rates of deforestation and forest degradation within the programme area.

6. The project has three outputs:

   - The first one is to enable REDD+ pilot implementation through three principal means:
     * Establishing a financing environment for forest sector transformation;
     * Reviving, strengthening the legal and regulatory framework for forestry; and
     * Improving the enforcement of the new regulatory framework.
   
   - The second output will:

     * Target the agricultural sector (including agroforestry) as a key driver of deforestation;


---
**Page 125**
---

### Lower targeted barriers;
- Enhance productivity;
- Increase farmers' integration into agricultural value chains;
- Improve access to finance and private sector participation in deforestation-free agriculture;

The overall project output will include local incentives for good agricultural practices and agroforestry, catalyzing private sector investment in value chains and sustainable rural infrastructure and watershed management; and
- The project will also contribute to climate change mitigation through forestry focusing on village forests and production and conservation forests to allow landscape management over time.

5. Net annual average greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions from land-use change and forestry in the period 2005–2015 accounted for 26.4 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (MCO2e). The Lao People's Democratic Republic is a small emitter, with agriculture contributing a further 28 per cent.

6. For the last 10 years, the Lao People's Democratic Republic has progressed substantially towards meeting its national climate change mitigation commitments under the Kyoto Protocol level/reference emission level (REL/FREEL), the REDD+ pilot strategy, the strategic environmental assessment (SEA) process, and the development of a national GHG inventory framework, as well as the final acceptance of the emission reductions programme document with the Carbon Fund. The country has an ambitious nationally determined contribution (NDC) target to cover 70 per cent of its land cover.

- Project 1 mitigates 5.6 million tCO2e during its four-year implementation period at a cost of EUR 3,489,000 (2019–2023), and project 2 will mitigate 144.7 million tCO2e over its 20-year lifetime (implementation period: 2020–2039), at a cost to GEF of EUR 8,040,000.

- The Global Environment Facility Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) believes that the programmatic approach needs further development, as it will need substantial resources to account for the expected high level of uncertainty in the project's mitigation potential. The methodology used is based on the methodologies used for the preparation of the reference level, which was submitted and approved (in June 2018) by the Forest Carbon Partnership Facility (FCPF) Carbon Fund and is compliant with the FCPF Carbon Fund’s Technical Guidelines. The methodology uses a highly detailed tier-3 approach in the terminology of the International Panel on Climate Change. Detailed information about the project's reference level, including the methodologies used for the post assessment of the achieved GHG emissions reduction and removals within project implementation periods, as well as the methodologies to be applied under results-based payments (RBP) for the period 2019–2024, for which the reference level is valid.

- Even though the project is presented as a mitigation project, it does have adaptation metrics. The Lao People's Democratic Republic has significant forest and agricultural capacity that is highly vulnerable to climate change impacts. Indeed, forestry and agriculture are two of the five key sectors identified in the NDCs as being highly vulnerable to climate change impacts. These sectors also face high risks from extreme weather events such as cyclones and wildfires, a medium risk for extreme heat, and a low risk for water scarcity; there are specific climate change impacts identified for the regions to be targeted by this project.

[FCFP (2016), available at <https://www.forestcarbonpartnership.org/carbon-fund/methodological-framework>](Page 157)


---
**Page 126**
---

10. To deliver the outputs, project I will create a REDD-plus funding window divided into four sub-volumes, one for each of the four different beneficiary groups: (i) government agencies at national, provincial and district levels; (ii) villages (village development funds); (iii) civil society organizations (CSOs); and (iv) small- and medium-sized enterprises (SMEs). The project will have 100 direct local beneficiaries and 235,800 indirect beneficiaries in the villages in the northern Lao People’s Democratic Republic. SMEs will receive training in good agricultural practices and business management, which in turn, will support and provide markets to approximately 4,600 producers and provide employment opportunities for an additional 17,000 people.

11. Overall, the project presents high mitigation and adaptation impacts consistent with GCF objectives.


## Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

### Innovation
12. The project has several elements of innovation. The jurisdictional approach bridging agriculture, forestry and financial sectors, and business activities is critical to enable real land-use change and sustainable development in the region. The innovative nature of this project will also enable a sustainable agricultural economy ensuring markets and value chains, and diversifying income sources for rural households.

13. The REDD-plus funding window brings concrete benefits to local beneficiaries, incentivizing villages to develop village development plans with a vision for change. Rewarding private sector actors through the provision of financial incentives for adopting more sustainable agricultural practices and markets could potentially reduce the need to expand the agricultural frontier in the region.

14. The project is also working to bridge national strategies with regional realities, revising and strengthening the legal and regulatory framework for forestry that will be applied in turn in relevant sub-national jurisdictions.

15. The project will also demonstrate a paradigm shift by ensuring effective coordination with relevant projects in the same jurisdiction, making more effective use of resources and a more coherent approach to land-use change. For example, the project is providing new finance streams for forests and a REDD-plus financing model that could be scalable.

16. The proposal presents a well-designed theory of change, with consistent assumptions and long-term outcomes and outputs to deliver the proposed paradigm shift.


### Potential for knowledge and learning
17. The project has a well-developed knowledge management plan (Annex 22A) of the funding window and its four sub-volumes. In addition, it will provide relevant tools to enable policymakers, provincial governments and local communities to identify and understand the benefits of REDD-plus projects in their respective jurisdictions. The project will also emphasize the importance of comprehensive communication and the exchange of information between different stakeholders at all levels to ensure that lessons learned are properly applied. Collaboration with national universities will be sought by inviting them to participate in REDD-plus related activities.

18. The project will also be critical in ensuring the impact of the project. The project will develop training for trainers, at the district level, involving the local staff who conduct of extensionists of the District Agriculture and Forestry Offices (DAFOs),


---
**Page 127**
---

which will include capacity building after project completion. Special emphasis will be placed on information systems and mapping, training individuals who will support the use of remote sensing techniques to generate maps to support Provincial Offices of Forestry Inspection and District offices for Forestry Inspectors in preventing deforestation and forest degradation.

### 1.2.3 Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

- The project will contribute to creating a conducive environment for REDD+ in a systemic manner, involving RBP through support to the FCPF Emission Reductions Program.
- The project gives special attention to support for policy and regulatory reform in forestry and agriculture, including strengthening the existing provincial agricultural extension system, ensuring the delivery of support to the communities and the scalability of the project.

The REDD+ pilot funding window will be funded and operationalized with concrete target beneficiaries. This will be complemented with a new green credit line for deforestation-free agricultural production, which is expected to be ready by the end of this project (activity 1.3).

### 1.2.4 Contribution to the regulatory framework and policy

A strategic component of the project is to strengthen the policy and regulatory framework. The project will focus on six areas:

- Mainstreaming REDD+ into socioeconomic development plans
- Developing enabling policies (revisions, formulation, and drafting of relevant laws, decrees and regulations) to enable sustainable forest management and private sector investment in lowland agriculture-based development;
- Strengthening monitoring and enforcement,
- Supporting land-use planning and measures to improve tenure security; and
- Strengthening the forest and forest carbon monitoring, reporting and verification (MRV) system.

### 1.2.5 Scalability and replicability

There is substantial potential for scaling up and replicating the programme. Project 1 is beginning in three provinces that could later be scaled to three more provinces with projects 2 and 3 (the Lao People's Democratic Republic has 17 provinces), covering over 50 districts. The model can be replicated elsewhere if there are sufficient financial flows to allow its scalability, ensuring economies of scale.

The programme will mainstream REDD+ into national and provincial development planning, creating the possibility for scaling up and replication of the programme interventions in the country at large.

### 1.3 Sustainable development potential

#### Scale: High

#### Environmental and social co-benefits

Although this GCF project is clearly a mitigation (REDD+) initiative, it incorporates interventions that will promote climate change adaptation – notably enhanced land-use planning, improvements in irrigation infrastructure, reforestation of catchment areas and climate-smart agriculture. These linkages provide certain guarantees that the long-term effects


---
**Page 128**
---

**(environmental and social co-benefits) of the project measured against the backdrop of climate change scenarios and impact will remain positive in the future.**

25. The National REDD+ Strategy (2023) and Vision (2030) aim to improve the quality and extent of forests nationwide to provide economic, social and environmental benefits. The policies and strategies are designed to ensure that all stakeholders, including indigenous peoples, actively participate in the reduction of deforestation and degradation, and the promotion of forest management practices.

(a) Improve the quality of forests by naturally regenerating up to 6 million ha and planting trees in untouched forest area up to 500,000 ha as an integral component of a rural development strategy that includes sustainable land use planning, improved forest management, stable water supplies and the prevention of natural disasters (floods and landslides).

(h) Provide a sustainable flow of forest products for domestic consumption and to generate revenue through wood-processing sales and exports, contributing to livelihood improvement, increased income and foreign exchange earnings, while increasing direct and indirect employment.

(e) Preserve the many species and unique habitats which are, for different reasons, under threat.

(c) Conserve the environment, including protection of soil, conservation of watersheds and minimizing climate change.

### 1.3.2 Economic co-benefits

26. The exact results will depend on the selected projects in the jurisdictions involved. The economic benefits of conserving forests are varied, including the provision of materials and food, thus contributing to income generation. In terms of the financial scheme, the economic co-benefits will be realized through a combination of poor people's access to markets from different opportunities, including carbon sequestration and timber production and transformation.

27. By catalysing investment in value chains, the project will create economic benefits for SMEs that will in turn increase profits and job creation. The project estimates that 160 SMEs will result in an estimated total of EUR 35 million in revenue, with a potential additional USD 9 million in investment. These 160 SMEs will, in turn, support and provide markets for approximately 24,000 smallholder farmers.

Output 2 promotes the transition to sustainable agricultural practices. The project presents financial models for shifting from traditional rice and maize cultivations to sequential intercropping of cash crops such as coffee and cocoa. In addition, through the programme, through its promotion of shifts in cultivation to more forest-friendly approaches, it is estimated that 100,000 ha will be better managed and used, with an economic net present value provided to the local beneficiary in estimated at EUR 325 million (annex 3/b of the funding proposal).

### Needs of the recipient

#### Scale: High

## Vulnerability of the countries and vulnerable groups

1. The Lao People’s Democratic Republic is a landlocked country with a population of 6.8 million people. Its Human Development Index (HDI) is 0.601, ranking it 139 out of 189 globally. The country's economic growth has more recently been among the highest in Asia and Africa, but its economy remains heavily dependent on natural resources for livelihood purposes; respect for customary use of land, including for ancestral and spiritual purposes; and appropriate access to information, including the use of ethnic languages and simple imagery to accommodate low literacy rates.

2. Growth has been largely dependent on natural resources, and this has placed increasing pressure on the environment in recent years. The country is also vulnerable due to adverse impacts on environmental services as well as water availability from climate change. The Lao People’s Democratic Republic still depends on forests and waterways for income and nutrition. The Lao Government has recognized limitations of this economic model and is implementing reforms to drive a greener and more sustainable economy.

3. Many of the country's poorest people live in rural areas and are the most vulnerable to pollution, climate change, and natural disasters. They depend on forests and waterways for their livelihoods by diversifying income sources and involving the private sector to spur market developments – for instance, by promoting sustainable forest management and nature-based tourism.

4. In its second national communication to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (UNFCCC), the Lao People’s Democratic Republic has identified a capacity gap that is highly vulnerable to climate change impacts. The Economy and Environment Programme for Southeast Asia aims at the Lao People's Democratic Republic as one of the most climate-vulnerable countries in the region.

5. According to the proposal, by 2100, mean annual temperatures are projected to increase by 1.4–4.3°C under high emissions scenarios; the number of days of extreme rainfall are


---
**Page 130**
---

projection increase by about seven days from 1990 to 2100, and the days of heatwaves to increase from fewer than 10 in 1990 to about 170 days, on average, in 2100.

3a. Poor and marginalized groups disproportionately face climate risks, among them temperature increases and erratic rainfall, given that they are more exposed to such changes and generally have less capacity to adapt or cope with the impacts of a changing environment. Natural disasters have increased, and about three-quarters were climate-related (food and water insecurity, flooding, landslides, droughts). These regions are rich in natural resources, but has a high risk of river flooding, landslides, cyclones and wildfires.

3b. Project 1 address the three provinces of Bhojapuri, Saubhag and Luang Prabang in the northern part of Laos. The average annual temperature increase is projected to be highest in the country, exceeding the national average by 23 per cent. Food security is a major challenge for many households, with an estimated 40-50 per cent of rural households in the region accounted as food poor.

41. These provinces contain the districts with the highest rates of deforestation and forest degradation. The project will support reforestation programmes to restore degraded area and remainning forest areas most at risk of deforestation/forest degradation. In addition, the geographical distribution of these areas is such that it has a significant impact on climate change at the landscape level.

42. The project presents accurate information on climate change for the northern part of Laos, with precipitation increase of 6-8% (9°C relative to the historical average by 2030). Precipitation may increase slightly (11–15 mm annual average) but with greater temporal variability.

42. Land use and deforestation are the main drivers of emissions. Deforestation and forest degradation are at the centre of the globally recognised biodiversity hotspots. Supporting the Luang Prabang Biosphere Reserve, the project will contribute to the conservation of this biodiversity and for the Indochina region at large, which is rapidly being depleted of its natural resources.

### 1.4.2 The need for strengthening institutions and implementation capacity

43. The country's transition to a green growth model implies a behavioural change that needs to be supported by strong institutional frameworks.

44. The project will support the revision of the Forestry Law (No.06/NA, 2007) and the Land Law, including implementing decrees and regulations. The body of regulations is currently internally inconsistent with each other and not well understood by local authorities. There is a need to support the regulatory framework to create appropriate conditions for the implementation of forest management plans and ensure that land-use plans are effectively implemented in the provinces. The project will promote local regulatory frameworks, enabling positive relations with local communities and the adoption of sustainable practices.

45. GCF project 1 will work closely with the Japan International Cooperation Agency (JICA) and the World Bank to support the development of a harmonized MRV system (DoFs) on a harmonized MRV system, including the national forest inventory and the preparation of the Forest Reference Level (FRL) under the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change.

**Page 131**

The project will also support the DAFO agricultural extension system ensuring capacity-building in providing services after project completion.

### Absence of alternative sources of financing

- The persistent fiscal deficit has led to an increase in public debt and an elevated risk of debt distress.
- Total public debt was nearly USD 9 billion in 2016, or 59 per cent of gross domestic product (GDP).
- The government is committed to reducing the fiscal deficit by cutting non-priority spending and increasing revenue.
- Despite this situation, the Lao People's Democratic Republic has committed substantial domestic resources to participate in REDD+ plus and to transform its forest sector from a GEF enterprise fund into a national program.
- The country is heavily dependent on donor support, partly because collection levels of legally mandated forest taxes are low.

### Country ownership

#### Alignment with national climate strategy
- In recent years, the Lao Government has embarked on several reforms that provide a platform for REDD+ plus implementation. These include the 2015 Forest Law and its focus on the granting of new concessions for mining, eucalyptus and rubber investments, which remain in effect since 2013 to protect natural forests from unsustainable exploitation.
- In 2016, the government established a National REDD+ plus Task Force (NRTF) with the aim of aligning already demonstrated a significant impact and underlines the ongoing forest law, enforcement, governance and trade voluntary partnership agreement negotiations with the European Union.
- The adoption of the new Forest Law in 2015 was a major step forward for reform in land management, in paving the way for a new Land Law in 2019. The Forest Law (2015) is currently under revision to address the emerging domestic and international challenges related to REDD+ plus implementation.
- The Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry has been returned to one ministry, the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry (MAF), thereby promoting joined-up policy development and implementation.

### Engagement with civil society organizations and other relevant stakeholders

GIZ conducted two preliminary fact-finding scoping missions in 2018, including a high-level meeting with government representatives to ensure commitments to project development from the outset.

An inception workshop for national government partners was held on 5 October 2018, where the initial project structure was presented, as well as key considerations for activities and related implementation. The project structure has been developed with regular engagement from the national designated authority (MoRFE). The project team also met with representatives of several other key ministries, where the government has demonstrated strong ownership of the project concept.

CSOs have been consulted extensively, and at frequent intervals, during the project preparation phase. In total, 102 CSO organizations were involved in the scoping process, which only 24 per cent were women. Therefore, the project implementers will need to ensure that their activities are gender-sensitive and inclusive.

Other stakeholders involved in the design of the project include the Laos Women’s Union (at national, provincial and district levels), Village Focus International, Center for People and Forests, the forest law enforcement group, and the Lao National Commission on Human Rights.

CSOs represent a central beneficiary of project support at the local level and will be involved in the implementation of the project. They are expected to contribute to awareness-raising, knowledge management and communication, gender plan establishment and public-private sector dialogue to promote village-based agroforestry project activities. Furthermore, CSOs in each province will be responsible for managing the project’s grievance redress mechanism and provided with information brochures.

2. The project is part of the REED-plus RFP, which has been in place since 2004, demonstrating genuine commitment from the Laos Government and civil society stakeholders. As part of the national REED-plus program, the Laos Government has designed a comprehensive approach to forest landscape restoration that builds on lessons learned before and is currently in negotiations regarding the emissions reduction payments agreement, through which it will receive financial incentives for its efforts.

3. The programmatic approach of the project will invest in the enabling environment to support deforestation-free agriculture and sustainable forest landscape management to receive results-based payments from the FCPF Carbon Fund. Moreover, the approach expects not only reverse deforestation but also to provide representation of the forest landscape on the ground, with a reversal follows sustained, uninterrupted and precipitous forest decline over the preceding in excess of 20 years.

4. The activities under outputs 2 and 3 are to promote the adoption of sustainable deforestation-free agriculture (such as intercropping or sequential cropping of upland rice and maize with leguminous crops) and sustainable forest landscape management (such that timber forest products and timber harvesting under approved guidelines) will result in positive reflexes for forests.

5. The project presents an estimated leverage impact annex that estimates to leverage EUR 227 million unlocking EUR 23 million from FCPF REED-plus RFP, additional carbon revenue is EUR 120 million from the ITF and other sources, including a USD 48 million loan from the World Bank credit line. EUR 77 million from increased agricultural productivity. EUR 86.6 million from protected areas and forest landscape restoration activities, and EUR 35.9 million in carbon revenue revenues. The ITAF believes that estimating these aggregated benefits is a unique way of presenting the impacts of actions from GCF resources.

6. The project will be implemented through the FCPF, with an amount of EUR 108,939 to beneficiaries that will be channeled through funding sub-windows. From this amount EUR 52,909 will be delivered in the form of direct payments to farmers and other stakeholders for the provision of goods and services (equipment, training, etc.) to various governmental agencies. These payments will be of three types: operational funds, upfront payments and performance-based payments. Beneficiaries will also receive a total amount of EUR 56,030 in cash transfers.

7. The resources will be channeled initially through the FPF and will progressively be transferred to the Forest Fund (FF) as the project progresses. It is not clear on this scheme and needs better refinement.

8. For the Laos Government window, the FF will channel funds to government ministries (principal departments (MOPs)), as well as their provincial and district counterparts (i.e., Provincial Offices of Natural Resources and Environment and District Offices of Natural Resources and Environment).

9. Government agencies at the national and subnational levels will be required to submit funding proposals to the REED-plus funding window in order to receive resources. The NPMU within the FPF will provide technical assistance to government agencies at all levels to prepare funding proposals.

**Page 136**

10. For the village window funds will be channelled to a deposit account of each village, a village development fund, and will be managed by Village Development Fund Committees. Villagers can apply to receive funds to invest in good agricultural practices aligned with an eligible agroforestry project. The funds will be provided on a performance-based basis using the fund management methods. Depending on the village, villagers will receive cash payments to implement the projects or they may receive services. Performance-based payments will be channelled to the village development funds as well.

11. For the private sector window (SMGs), there will be private sector village partnership agreements with a 50/50 cost-sharing arrangement between GCF and the SMG. The village window covering 50 per cent of the establishment costs for village-based agroforestry (up to EUR 2,000 per SMG) will be provided by GCF. The remaining 50 per cent will be financed by the enterprises, as per the private sector village partnership agreement.

12. The ITAP observed in the submission that the fees paid to international consultants were very high and that a significant portion of the budget allocated for the mobilization of resources was destined for consultants rather than beneficiaries. Based on this observation, GCF decided to reduce the consultant fees by 50 per cent and to reallocate more GCF resources to increase the funding of the REIO plus funding window.

13. GIZ is investing in the development of a green credit line to boost agricultural productivity and sustainable land management (SLM) practices through financing to SMEs (with a particular focus on women-led SMEs). GIZ will not use GCF funds to finance lending under this credit line, but rather intends to use the proceeds from the credit line to mobilize international public and private finance to capitalize the green credit line. The selection of a partner financial institution to operate the green credit line is under discussion involving the GIZ team in charge of the project, the GCF Secretariat, and the Banque Le Commerce Extérieur Luxo.

14. Finally, there was an economic model that takes into consideration the reduction of CO2 emissions and economic value-added captured by project beneficiaries. Assuming as a base case, a carbon price of EUR 25/tCO2eq (the European Union Emissions Trading Scheme price at the time of writing) and applying this to the estimated annual sequestration per hectare findings of the financial analysis above the entire target area of output 2 and 3, the program would have an internal rate of return of 10.5 per cent and a Net Present Value (NPV) of EUR 1,485 million (over a 20-year period and a 10 per cent discount rate).

## Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

The ITAP recommends approval of this project.

The ITAP notes that the project involves many actors and layers that will be difficult to coordinate. The ITAP recommends providing a well-structured benefit-sharing mechanism with concrete rules for its implementation, as well as ensuring that all relevant governance mechanisms, at the national level and also at the local level where all the different players coincide.
